\mathcal{M}^{\max}_{[c]}

Lemma 13
.

Algorithm
4.1
is sound and runs in polynomial time.

Proof 4.3
.

First, we show the soundness of Algorithm
4.1
.
By the virtue of Lemma
10
stating the soundness of the transformation
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{M}_{[c]}
it
suffices to show that Algorithm
4.1
returns the correct
answers “yes” or “no” when the task is to check whether the
singleton
\mathsf{Cause}=\{c\}
is an SPR cause in
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}_{[c]}
.
Recall the notation
q_{s}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}_{[c]},s}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
.
We abbreviate
q=q_{\mathsf{init}}
.
Note that
(\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c
is equivalent to
\Diamond c
as
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
.

For every scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathcal{N}
we have

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N},c}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=w_{c}
.
Thus,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\ \Diamond c)=w_{c}

if
\mathfrak{S}
is a scheduler of
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond c)>0
.

Algorithm
4.1
correctly answers “no” (case 2 or 3.1)
if
w_{c}=0
.
Suppose that
w_{c}>0
.
Thus, the SPR condition for
c
reduces to
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})<w_{c}
for all schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond c)>0
.

1.

of Algorithm
4.1
(i.e., if
q<w_{c}
), the answer “yes” is sound
as
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=q<w_{c}
.

2.

(i.e., if
q>w_{c}
)
Let
\mathfrak{T}
be an MD-scheduler
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N},s}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=q_{s}

for each state
s
and pick an MD-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond c)>0
.
It is no restriction to suppose that

\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{S}
realize the same end components of
\mathcal{N}
.
(Note that if state
s
belongs to an end component
that is realized by
\mathfrak{T}
then
s
contained in a bottom strongly
connected component of the Markov chain induced by
\mathfrak{T}
.
But then

q_{s}=0
, i.e., no effect state is reachable from
s
in
\mathcal{N}
.
Recall that all effect states are terminal and thus not contained in
end components.
But then we can safely assume that
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{S}
schedule
the same action for state
s
.)
Let
\lambda
be any real number with
1>\lambda>\frac{w_{c}}{q}

and let
\mathcal{K}
denote the sub-MDP of
\mathcal{N}
with state space
S

where the enabled
actions of state
s
are the actions scheduled for
s
under one of the
schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
or
\mathfrak{S}
.
Let now
\mathfrak{U}
be the MR-scheduler

\lambda\mathfrak{T}\oplus(1{-}\lambda)\mathfrak{S}

defined as in Lemma
2

for the EC-free MDP resulting from
\mathcal{K}
when collapsing

\mathcal{K}
’s end components into a single terminal state.
For the states belonging to an end component of
\mathcal{K}
,

\mathfrak{U}
schedules the same action as
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{S}
.
Then,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond t)=\lambda\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond t)+(1{-}\lambda)\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond t)

for all terminal states
t
of
\mathcal{N}
and
t=c
.
Hence:

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\Diamond c)\ \ \geqslant\ \ (1{-}\lambda)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)\ \ >\ \ 0

and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ \ \geqslant\ \ \lambda\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ \ =\ \ \lambda\cdot q\ \ >\ \ w_{c}

Thus, scheduler
\mathfrak{U}
is a witness
why (
SPR
) does not hold for
c
.

3.1

Pick an MD-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathcal{M}^{\max}_{[c]}
such that

c
is reachable from
\mathsf{init}
via
\mathfrak{S}
and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N},s}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=q_{s}
for all states.
Thus, (
SPR
) does not hold for
c
and scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
.

3.2

We have
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond c)=0
for all schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{N}

with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=q=w_{c}
.
But then
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond c)>0
implies

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})<w_{c}

as required in (
SPR
).

The polynomial runtime of Algorithm
4.1

follows from the fact that minimal and maximal reachability probabilities and hence also the MDPs
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}_{[c]}
and its sub-MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\max}_{[c]}
can be computed in polynomial time.

By applying Algorithm
4.1
to all states
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
and standard algorithms to check the existence of a path satisfying
(\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c
for every state
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, we obtain:

Theorem 14

(Checking SPR causes)
.

The problem “given
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
and
\mathsf{Eff}
, check whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
” is solvable in polynomial-time.

Remark 15

(Memory requirements for (S))
.

As the soundness proof for Algorithm
4.1

shows:
If
\mathsf{Cause}
does not satisfy (S),
then there is an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for

\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
witnessing the violation of (
SPR
).
Scheduler
\mathfrak{S}

corresponds to a finite-memory (randomized) scheduler
\mathfrak{T}

with two memory cells for
\mathcal{M}
:
“before
\mathsf{Cause}
” (where
\mathfrak{T}
behaves as
\mathfrak{S}
)
and “after
\mathsf{Cause}
” (where
\mathfrak{T}
behaves as an MD-scheduler minimizing
the effect probability).

\lhd

Lemma 16

(Criterion for the existence of PR causes)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\mathsf{Eff}
a nonempty set of states. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

\mathsf{Eff}
has an SPR cause in
\mathcal{M}
,

(b)

\mathsf{Eff}
has a GPR cause in
\mathcal{M}
,

(c)

there is a state
c_{0}\in S\setminus\mathsf{Eff}
such that
the singleton
\{c_{0}\}
is an SPR cause (and therefore a GPR cause) for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

Thus, the existence of SPR and GPR causes can be checked
with Algorithm
4.1
in polynomial time.

Proof 4.4
.

Obviously, statement (c) implies statements (a) and (b).
The implication “(a)
\Longrightarrow
(b)” follows from
Lemma
5
.
We now turn to the proof of “(b)
\Longrightarrow
(c)”.
For this, we assume that we are given a
GPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
For
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, let
w_{c}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M},c}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
.
Pick a state
c_{0}\in\mathsf{Cause}
such that

w_{c_{0}}=\max\{w_{c}:c\in\mathsf{Cause}\}
.
For every scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
that minimizes the
effect probability whenever it visits a state in
\mathsf{Cause}
,
and visits
\mathsf{Cause}
with positive probability,
the conditional probability

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}|\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})
is
a weighted average of the values
w_{c}
,
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
,
and thus bounded by
w_{c_{0}}
.
Using Lemma
10
we see that it is sufficient to only consider the minimal probabilities
w_{c}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M},c}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
.
Thus, we conclude that
\{c_{0}\}
is both an SPR and a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
.

4.2.
Checking the global probability-raising condition

Throughout this section, we suppose that both the effect set
\mathsf{Eff}
and the cause candidate
\mathsf{Cause}
are fixed disjoint subsets of the state space of the MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
, and address the task to check whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a global probability-raising cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
. As the minimality condition (for all
c\in\mathsf{Cause}:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0)
can be checked in polynomial time using a standard graph algorithm, we will concentrate on an algorithm to check the probability-raising condition (
GPR
).
We start by stating the main results of this section.

Theorem 17
.

Given
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
and
\mathsf{Eff}
, deciding whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
can be done in
\mathrm{coNP}
.

In order to provide an algorithm, we perform a model transformation after which the violation of (
GPR
) by a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
can be expressed solely in terms of the expected frequencies of the state-action pairs of the transformed MDP under
\mathfrak{S}
.
This allows us to express the existence of a scheduler witnessing the non-causality of
\mathsf{Cause}
in terms of the satisfiability of a quadratic constraint system.
Thus, we can restrict the quantification in (G) to MR-schedulers in the transformed model.
We trace back the memory requirements to
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
and to the original MDP
\mathcal{M}
yielding the second main result.

Theorem 18
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP with effect set
\mathsf{Eff}
as before and

\mathsf{Cause}
a set of non-effect states which satisfies for all
c\in\mathsf{Cause}:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
.
If
\mathsf{Cause}
is not a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
, then
there is an MR-scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
refuting the GPR
condition for
\mathsf{Cause}

in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}

and a finite-memory scheduler for
\mathcal{M}
with two memory cells
refuting the GPR
condition for
\mathsf{Cause}

in
\mathcal{M}
.

The remainder of this section is concerned with the proofs of both
Theorem
17
and
Theorem
18
.
For this, we suppose that
\mathsf{Cause}
satisfies for all
c\in\mathsf{Cause}:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
which can be checked preemptively in polynomial time as argued before.

Checking the GPR condition (Proof of Theorem
17
).

We will start with a polynomial-time model transformation into a kind of “canonical form” after which we can make the following assumptions when checking the GPR condition of
\mathsf{Cause}
for
\mathsf{Eff}

(A1):

\mathsf{Eff}=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}\}
consists of two terminal states.

(A2):

For every
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, there is a single enabled
action
\mathit{Act}(c)=\{\gamma\}
, and
there is
w_{c}\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
such that

P(c,\gamma,\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})=w_{c}
and

P(c,\gamma,\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})=1{-}w_{c}
,
where
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
is a terminal non-effect state
and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
are only accessible via

\gamma
-transition from the
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
.

(A3):

\mathcal{M}
has no end components and
there is a further terminal state
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}

and an action
\tau
such that

\tau\in\mathit{Act}(s)
implies
P(s,\tau,\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}})=1
.

The terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
,
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}

are pairwise distinct.
\mathcal{M}
can have further terminal
states representing true negatives.
However, these can be identified with
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
.

Intuitively,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
stands for covered effects (“
\mathsf{Eff}
after
\mathsf{Cause}
”)
and can be seen as a true positive,
while
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
represents the uncovered effects (“
\mathsf{Eff}
without preceding
\mathsf{Cause}
”)
and corresponds to a false negative.
Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler in
\mathcal{M}
. Note that

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,\mathsf{Eff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})

and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})
.
As the cause states can not reach each other we also have

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c)=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)

for each
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
.
The intuitive meaning of
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
is a false positive (“no effect after
\mathsf{Cause}
”), while
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
stands for true negatives where neither the effect nor the cause is observed.
Note that

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})

and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}})
.

Establishing assumptions (A1)-(A3):

We justify the assumptions as we can transform
\mathcal{M}
into a new MDP of the same asymptotic size satisfying the above assumptions.
Thanks to Lemma
10
,
we may suppose that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
without changing the satisfaction of (G).
Thus, from cause states
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
there are only two outgoing transitions, either to a terminal effect state
\mathsf{eff}
with probability
\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{c}(\lozenge\mathsf{Eff})
or to a terminal non-effect state
\mathsf{noeff}
with the remaining probability (see Notation
4
).
We then may rename the effect state
\mathsf{eff}
and the non-effect state
\mathsf{noeff}
reachable from
\mathsf{Cause}
into
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
, respectively.
Furthermore, we collapse all other effect states into a single state
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
and all true negative states into a single state
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
.
Similarly, by renaming and possibly duplicating terminal states we also suppose that
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
has no other incoming transitions than the
\gamma
-transitions from the states in
\mathsf{Cause}
.
This ensures (A1) and (A2).
For (A3) consider the set
T
of terminal states in the MDP obtained so far.
We remove all non-trivial end components by switching to the MEC-quotient
[
dA97
]
, i.e., we collapse all states that belong to the same MEC
\mathcal{E}
into a single state
s_{\mathcal{E}}
representing the MEC while ignoring the actions inside
\mathcal{E}
.
Additionally, we add a fresh deterministic
\tau
-transition from the states
s_{\mathcal{E}}
representing MECs to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}

(i.e.,
P(s_{\mathcal{E}},\tau,\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}})=1
).
The
\tau
-transitions from states
s_{\mathcal{E}}
to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
can then be used to mimic cases where the scheduler of the original MDP enters the end component
\mathcal{E}
and stays there forever.

In particular, consider the MEC-quotient
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
(see Definition
2.3
).
Let
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
be the state to which we add a
\tau
-transition with probability
1
from each MEC that we collapse in the MEC-quotient.
That is,
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}=\bot
with the notations of Definition
2.3
.

We demonstrate these transformations on the abstract MDP
\mathcal{M}
from Figure
12
, where the dotted circles correspond to sets of states in the MDP.
The MDP already satisfies
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{[c]}
.
We rename
\mathsf{eff}
reachable from
\mathsf{Cause}
to
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
\mathsf{noeff}
to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
.
Effect states not reachable from
\mathsf{Cause}
collapse to
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
.
There are no terminal non-effect states not reachable from
c
, which would collapse to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
.
The MEC quotient collapses MECs to states
s_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
only keeping outgoing transitions.
There is a fresh action
\tau
in states
s_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
.
Thus, we get
\mathcal{N}
from Figure
12
.

\mathsf{init}
text
MDP
a
a
a
\mathsf{MEC}
\mathsf{MEC}
\mathsf{Cause}
\mathsf{eff}
\mathsf{noeff}
\mathsf{eff}

\beta

Figure 11.
MDP
\mathcal{M}
not satisfying

assumptions (A1)-(A3)

\mathsf{init}

a

\mathsf{Cause}

Transformed MDP
\mathcal{N}
satisfying

assumptions (A1)-(A3)

The following Lemma
19
and Corollary
20
prove the soundness of the model transformation.

Lemma 19
.

For each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{N}
, and vice versa, such that

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})
, and

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\land\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})
.

Proof 4.5
.

By Lemma
3
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{N}
for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
such that each terminal state is reached with the same probability under
\mathfrak{T}
in
\mathcal{N}
and under
\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
.
The state
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
is present in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
under the name
\mathsf{eff}
.
The state
\mathsf{eff}
is furthermore reached in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
if and only if
\Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\land\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}
is satisfied along a run.
The set of terminal states in
\mathsf{Eff}
is obtained from the set
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
by collapsing states. As a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
can be viewed as a scheduler for both MDPs and these MDPs agree except for the terminal states, the first equality follows as well.
As the probability to reach
\mathsf{Cause}
is the sum of the probabilities to reach the terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
in
\mathcal{N}
and as these states are only renamed in
\mathcal{N}
in comparison to
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
, the claim follows.

From Lemma
19
and Lemma
10
, we conclude the following corollary that justifies working under assumptions (A1)-(A3).

Corollary 20
.

The set
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof 4.6
.

By Lemma
19
, for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{N}
such that all relevant probabilities agree, and vice versa. So,
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
if and only if it is a GPR cause in
\mathcal{N}
.
By Lemma
10
,

\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
if and only if it is a GPR cause in
\mathcal{M}
.

Note, however, that the transformation changes the memory-requirements of schedulers witnessing that
\mathsf{Cause}
is not a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
. We will address the memory requirements in the original MDP later.
With assumptions (A1)-(A3), condition (G) can be reformulated as follows:

Lemma 21
.

Under assumptions (A1)-(A3),
\mathsf{Cause}
satisfies (G)
if and only if for
each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0

the following condition holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})\ <\ \bigl(1{-}\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\bigr)\cdot\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)\cdot w_{c}

(GPR-1)

With assumptions (A1)-(A3), a terminal state of
\mathcal{M}
is reached almost surely under any scheduler after finitely many steps in expectation.
Given a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
recall the definition of expected frequencies of
state action-pairs
(s,\alpha)
, states
s\in S
and state-sets
T\subseteq S

under
\mathfrak{S}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s,\alpha)

\displaystyle\ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ \ \mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\text{number of visits to $s$ in which $\alpha$ is taken})

\displaystyle\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s)

\displaystyle\ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum\nolimits_{\alpha\in\mathit{Act}(s)}\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s,\alpha),\qquad\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum\nolimits_{s\in T}\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s).

Let
T
be one of the sets
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}\}
,
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
, or a singleton
\{c\}
with
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
.
As
T
is visited at most once during each run of
\mathcal{M}
(assumptions (A1) and (A2)), we have

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond T)=\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(T)

for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
.
This allows us to express the violation of (G) in terms of a quadratic constraint system over variables for the expected frequencies of state-action pairs.
Let
\mathit{StAct}
denote the set of state-action pairs in
\mathcal{M}
. We consider the following constraint system over the variables
x_{s,\alpha}
for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}
where we use the short form notation

x_{s}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathit{Act}(s)}x_{s,\alpha}
:

\displaystyle x_{s,\alpha}

\displaystyle\ \geqslant\ 0\qquad\text{for all $(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}$}

(S1)

\displaystyle x_{\mathsf{init}}

\displaystyle\ =\ 1+\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(t,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}}\!\!\!\!\!x_{t,\alpha}\cdot P(t,\alpha,\mathsf{init})

(S2)

\displaystyle x_{s}

\displaystyle\ =\sum_{(t,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}}\!\!\!\!\!x_{t,\alpha}\cdot P(t,\alpha,s)\qquad\text{for all $s\in S\setminus\{\mathsf{init}\}$}

(S3)

Using well-known results for MDPs without ECs (see, e.g.,
[
Kal20
, Theorem 9.16]
), given a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathit{StAct}}
, then
x
is a solution to (S1) and the balance equations (S2) and (S3)
if and only if there is a (possibly history-dependent) scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
with
x_{s,\alpha}=\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s,\alpha)
for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}

if and only if there is an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
with
x_{s,\alpha}=\mathit{freq}_{\mathfrak{S}}(s,\alpha)
for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}
.

The violation of (
GPR-1
) in Lemma
21

and the condition
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0

can be reformulated in terms of the frequency-variables as follows where
x_{\mathsf{Cause}}
is an abbreviation for
\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}x_{c}
:

\displaystyle 0\ \ \geqslant\ \ \bigl(1-x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\bigl)\cdot\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!x_{c}\cdot w_{c}\ -\ x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\cdot x_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}

(S4)

\displaystyle x_{\mathsf{Cause}}>0

(S5)

Lemma 22
.

Under assumptions (A1)-(A3), the set
\mathsf{Cause}
is not a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
iff the constructed quadratic system of inequalities (S1)-(S5) has a solution.

We can now prove our first main Theorem
17
of this subsection, stating that deciding the GPR condition can be done in
\mathrm{coNP}
.

Proof 4.7

(Proof of Theorem
17
)
.

The quadratic system of inequalities can be constructed from
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
, and
\mathsf{Eff}
in polynomial time.
Except for the strict inequality constraint in (S5), it has the form of a quadratic program, for which the threshold problem can be decided in
\mathrm{NP}
by
[
Vav90
]
.
We will prove that also with this strict inequality, it can be checked in
\mathrm{NP}
whether the system (S1)-(S5) has a solution.
As the system of inequalities is expressing the violation of (
GPR
), deciding whether a set
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR cause can then be done in
\mathrm{coNP}
.

To show that satisfiability of the system (S1)-(S5) is in
\mathrm{NP}
, we will provide a non-deterministic algorithm that runs in polynomial time and finds a solution if one exists.
Some of the arguments are similar to the arguments used in
[
Vav90
]
. Additionally, we will rely on the implicit function theorem.

We begin by proving what a solution to (S1)-(S5) can be assumed to look like.
Thus assume that a solution to (S1)-(S5) exists.
There are two possible cases:

Case 1:

All solutions to (S1)-(S3) and (S5) satisfy (S4). Then, in particular, the frequency values of an MD-scheduler maximizing the probability to reach Cause are a solution to (S1)-(S3) and (S5) and hence to (S4) in this case.

Case 2:

There are solutions to (S1)-(S3) and (S5) that violate (S4). The space of feasible points for conditions (S1)-(S3) and (S5) is connected.
The right hand side of (S4)

\bigl(1-x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\bigl)\cdot\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!x_{c}\cdot w_{c}\ -\ x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\cdot x_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}

is continuous. Hence, as there are also solutions to (S1)-(S3) and (S5) that satisfy (S4) by assumption, there is a solution to (S1)-(S3) and (S5) that satisfies

\bigl(1-x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\bigl)\cdot\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!x_{c}\cdot w_{c}\ -\ x_{\mathsf{Cause}}\cdot x_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}=0.

Now, let us take a closer look at Case 2:
First of all, we add the equation

x_{\mathsf{Cause}}=\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}x_{c}

to our system.
Thus, the variables are
x_{\mathsf{Cause}}
and
x_{s,\alpha}
for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}
.
Obviously, this does not influence the satisfiability.
Equation (S4’) now contains the new variable
x_{\mathsf{Cause}}
, which is not an abbreviation anymore.
We write
x
for the vector of variables
x_{s,\alpha}
with
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{StAct}
.

In Case 2, there is a solution
(x^{\ast},x_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
such that the maximal possible number of variables is
0
and such that
x_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast}
is maximal among all such solutions.
Let
X^{\prime}
be the set of variables that are
0
in
(x^{\ast},x_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.
We remove all variables from
X^{\prime}
from all constraints by setting them to
0

and call the resulting system (T1)-(T6) where (T4) is obtained from (S4’), while all other equations (T
i
) are obtained from (S
i
), by removing the chosen variables.
We then collect the remaining variables in the vector
v=(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})
.
Let
(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
be the solution
(x^{\ast},x_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
after the variables in
X^{\prime}
have been removed.
Thus, all values in this vector are positive.

Define the function
f
as the right hand side of (T4):

f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})=\bigl(1-y_{\mathsf{Cause}}\bigl)\cdot\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!y_{c}\cdot w_{c}\ -\ y_{\mathsf{Cause}}\cdot y_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}},

where the variables
y
are as the original variables
x
after the variables in
X^{\prime}
have been removed.

Now, we apply the implicit function theorem:
Observe that

\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial y_{\mathsf{Cause}}}=-\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}y_{c}\cdot w_{c}-y_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}.

Evaluated at
(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
, this value is non-zero as all summands are negative and there are at least some of the variables in the abbreviation
y_{c}
with
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
left, i.e., not removed because they were not
0
due to the original constraint (S5).
So, we can apply the implicit function theorem, which guarantees us the existence of a function
g(y)
, such that
g(y^{\ast})=y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast}
and, for all
y^{\prime}
in an open ball
B_{1}
around
y^{\ast}
, we have

f(y^{\prime},g(y^{\prime}))=0.

By the implicit function theorem, we can explicitly compute the gradient

\nabla g=\left(\frac{\partial g(y)}{\partial{y_{1}}},\dots,\frac{\partial g(y)}{\partial{y_{k}}}\right)=-\left(\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial y_{\mathsf{Cause}}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial{y_{1}}},\dots,\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial{y_{k}}}\right)

of the derivatives on
B_{1}

for the appropriate
k
from the derivatives of
f
.
Note that on
B_{1}
, the gradient
\nabla g
is
0
iff

H(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left(\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial{y_{1}}},\dots,\frac{\partial f(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})}{\partial{y_{k}}}\right)

is
0
. Furthermore, all entries of
H(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})
are linear in the variables
v
as the function
f
is quadratic.
As the function
g
has a local maximum in
y^{\ast}
, we know that
\nabla g
evaluated at
y^{\ast}
is
0
.

Equations (T2), (T3), and (T6) are linear equations in the remaining variables
v
.
We can rewrite these three equations with a matrix
M
and a vector
b
whose entries can easily be expressed in terms of the coefficients of the original system (again, after the set of variables
X^{\prime}
has been removed) as

Mv=b.

The solutions to this equation form an
r
-dimensional affine space
W
. It can be written as

W=\{\underbrace{c_{0}+c_{1}\cdot z_{1}+\dots+c_{r}\cdot z_{r}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}h(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})}\mid(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\}

for some vectors
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{r}
which can be computed from
M
and
b
in polynomial time.

Let
B_{2}
be an open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that
h(B_{2})\subseteq B_{1}
and such that
h(B_{2})
contains
(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.
We claim that
g\circ h\colon B_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
has an isolated local maximum at
z^{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}h^{-1}(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.
It is clear that
g\circ h
has a local maximum since
g
has a local maximum at
(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.
Suppose now, that
g\circ h
does not have an isolated local maximum at
h^{-1}(y^{\ast},y_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.
As
h
is an affine map and the graph of
g
is the solution to a quadratic equation, this is only possible if there is a direction
d\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\displaystyle g\circ h(z^{\ast})=g\circ h(z^{\ast}+t\cdot d)

(6)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Due to the boundedness of the polyhedron described by conditions (T1)-(T3), (T5) and (T6) and since
z^{\ast}
lies in the interior of this polyhedron, this means that there must be a value
q\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(h(z^{\ast}+q\cdot d),g\circ h(z^{\ast}+q\cdot d))
provides a solution
v
to equations (T1)-(T3), (T5) and (T6) with an additional
0
.
By the definition of
g
, this solution furthermore satisfies (T4) and, by equation (
6
), it still satisfies (T5). This contradicts the choice of the original
solution
(x^{\ast},x_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\ast})
.

So,
g\circ h\colon B_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
has an isolated local maximum at
z^{\ast}
. This implies that on an open ball around
z^{\ast}
, the point
z^{\ast}
is the only solution to

\nabla g(h(z))=0

and consequently, the only solution to

H(h(z))=0.

Since
H(h(z))
is a vector of linear expressions in
z
, this implies that
z^{\ast}
is the only solution on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
to
H(h(z))=0
.
This is the key result that we need to provide a non-deterministic polynomial-time algorithm to check the satisfiability of the original constraint system.

Let us now describe the algorithm:
The algorithm begins by computing the frequency values of an MD-scheduler as in Case 1 in polynomial time and checks whether the resulting vector of frequency values satisfies (S1)-(S5).
If this is the case, the algorithm returns that the system is satisfiable.

If this is not the case, the algorithm tries to compute a solution to (S1)-(S3), (S5), and (S4’) as in Case 2.
The algorithm non-deterministically guesses a subset of the variables and removes them
from all constraints by replacing them with
0
.

Suppose we guess the set
X^{\prime}
as above.
We show that we then compute a solution.
After the variables from
X^{\prime}
have been removed,
H(y,y_{\mathsf{Cause}})
can be computed in polynomial time as all the derivatives of
f
are linear expressions in the variables which require basic arithmetic and can be computed in polynomial time.
Likewise,
M
and
b
can be computed in polynomial time from the original constraints after the guessed variables have been removed.
The vectors
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{r}
describing the solution space to
Mv=b
can then also be computed in polynomial time.

Thus, also the vector
H(h(z))
of linear expressions in the variables
z
can be computed in polynomial time.
The equation system
H(h(z))=0
has a unique solution if the guessed variables were indeed
X^{\prime}
.
In this case, the solution
z^{\ast}
can be computed in polynomial time as well.
If the guess of variables was not
X^{\prime}
, then either there is no unique solution to this equation system which can be detected in polynomial time, or the solution, which is computed in the sequel in polynomial time, might not satisfy the original constraints, which is checked in the end.

From
z^{\ast}
, we can compute
y^{\ast}=h(z^{\ast})
using the vectors
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{r}
. The solution

x^{\ast}
is then obtained by plugging in
0
s for the removed variables. Checking whether the resulting vector satisfies all constraints can also be done in polynomial time in the end.
If
X^{\prime}
was guessed correctly, this vector
x^{\ast}
indeed forms a solution to the original constraints as we have seen.

In summary, the algorithm needs to guess the set
X^{\prime}
of variables which are
0
in a solution to the original constraints with the maximal number of zeroes.
All other steps are deterministic polynomial-time computations.
Thus, satisfiability of (S1)-(S5) can be checked in NP.

Memory requirements of schedulers in the original MDP (Proof of Theorem
18
).

Every solution to the
linear system of inequalities (S1), (S2), and (S3) corresponds to the
expected frequencies of state-action pairs of an MR-scheduler in the
transformed model satisfying (A1)-(A3). Hence:

Corollary 23
.

Under assumptions (A1)-(A3),

\mathsf{Cause}
is no GPR cause for

\mathsf{Eff}
iff there exists an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{T}

with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0

violating (
GPR
).

The model transformation we used for assumptions (A1)-(A3), however, does affect the memory requirements of a violating scheduler.
We may further restrict the MR-schedulers necessary to witness non-causality under assumptions (A1)-(A3).
For the following lemma, recall that
\tau
is the action of the MEC quotient used for the extra transition from states representing MECs to a new trap state (see also assumption (A3)).

Lemma 24
.

Assume (A1)-(A3).
Given an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{U}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
that violates (
GPR
),
an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
with
\mathfrak{T}(s)(\tau)\in\{0,1\}
for each state
s
with
\tau\in\mathit{Act}(s)

that satifies
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
and violates (
GPR
)
is computable in polynomial time.

Proof 4.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{U}
be a scheduler with

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
violating (
GPR-1
), i.e.:

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})\ <\ \bigl(1{-}\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\bigr)\cdot\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)\cdot w_{c}.

We will show how to transform
\mathfrak{U}
into an
MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
that schedules the
\tau
-transitions to

\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
with probability 0 or 1.
We regard the set
U
of states

u
which have a
\tau
-transition to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}

(recall that then
P(u,\tau,\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}})=1
)
and where
0<\mathfrak{U}(u)(\tau)<1
.
We now process the
U
-states in an arbitrary order,
say
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
,
and generate a sequence
\mathfrak{T}_{0}=\mathfrak{U},\mathfrak{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{T}_{k}

of MR-schedulers such that for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
:

•

\mathfrak{T}_{i}
refutes (
GPR
)
(or equivalently condition (
GPR-1
) from
Lemma
21
)

•

\mathfrak{T}_{i}

agrees with
\mathfrak{T}_{i-1}
for all states but
u_{i}
,

•

\mathfrak{T}_{i}(u_{i})(\tau)\in\{0,1\}
.

Thus, the final scheduler
\mathfrak{T}_{k}
satisfies the
desired properties.

To explain how to derive
\mathfrak{T}_{i}
from
\mathfrak{T}_{i-1}
,
let
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
\mathfrak{V}=\mathfrak{T}_{i-1}
,
u=u_{i}

and
y=1{-}\mathfrak{V}(u)(\tau)
. By definition we have,
0<y<1
(as
u\in U
and
by definition of
U
) and

y=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathit{Act}(u)\setminus\{\tau\}}\mathfrak{V}(u)(\alpha)
.
For
x\in[0,1]
, let
\mathfrak{V}_{x}
denote the MR-scheduler that
agrees with
\mathfrak{V}
for all states but
u
, for which

\mathfrak{V}_{x}
’s decision is:

\mathfrak{V}_{x}(u)(\tau)=1{-}x,\qquad\mathfrak{V}_{x}(u)(\alpha)=\mathfrak{V}(u)(\alpha)\cdot\frac{x}{y}\quad\text{for $\alpha\in\mathit{Act}(u)\setminus\{\tau\}$}

Obviously,
\mathfrak{V}_{y}=\mathfrak{V}
.
We now show that at least one of the two MR-schedulers

\mathfrak{V}_{0}
or
\mathfrak{V}_{1}
also refutes (
GPR
).
For this, we suppose by contraction that this is not the case,
which means that (
GPR
) holds for both. Let
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{Q}
be defined by

f(x)\ =\ \mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})-\bigl(1{-}\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\bigr)\cdot\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)\cdot w_{c}

As
\mathfrak{V}=\mathfrak{V}_{y}
violates (
GPR-1
),
while
\mathfrak{V}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{V}_{1}

satisfy (
GPR-1
) we obtain:

f(0),f(1)<0\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(y)\geqslant 0

We now split
\mathsf{Cause}
into the set
C
of states
c\in\mathsf{Cause}

such that there is a
\mathfrak{V}
-path from
\mathsf{init}
to
c
that traverses

u
and
D=\mathsf{Cause}\setminus C
.
Thus,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})=p_{x}+p

where
p_{x}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}(\Diamond C)
and

p=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}}(\Diamond D)
.
Similarly,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})
has the form

q_{x}+q
where

q_{x}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(u\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}))

and

q=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg u)\,\mathrm{U}\,\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})
.
With
p_{x,c}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}_{x}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond c)

for
c\in C

and
p_{d}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{V}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond d)
for
d\in D
,
let

v_{x}=\sum_{c\in C}p_{x,c}\cdot w_{c}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v=\sum_{d\in D}p_{d}\cdot w_{d}

As
y
is fixed, the values

p_{y},p_{y,c},q_{y},v_{y}
can be seen as
constants.
Moreover,
p_{x},p_{x,c},q_{x},v_{x}
differ from

p_{y},p_{y,c},q_{y},v_{y}

only by the factor
\frac{x}{y}
.
That is:

p_{x}=p_{y}\frac{x}{y}
,
p_{x,c}=p_{y,c}\frac{x}{y}
,
q_{x}=q_{y}\frac{x}{y}
and
v_{x}=v_{y}\frac{x}{y}
.

Thus,

f(x)
has the following form:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p_{x}{+}p)(q_{x}{+}q)-\bigl(1{-}(p_{x}{+}p)\bigr)(v_{x}{+}v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{p_{x}q_{x}{+}p_{x}v_{x}}_{\mathfrak{a}x^{2}}+\underbrace{p_{x}(q+v)+q_{x}p-v_{x}}_{\mathfrak{b}x}+\underbrace{pq-v+pv}_{\mathfrak{c}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}x^{2}+\mathfrak{b}x+\mathfrak{c}

For the value
\mathfrak{a}
, we have

\mathfrak{a}x^{2}=p_{x}q_{x}{+}p_{x}v_{x}
and hence

\mathfrak{a}=\frac{1}{y^{2}}(p_{y}q_{y}+p_{y}v_{y})>0
.
But then the second derivative
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=2\mathfrak{a}
of

f
is positive, which yields
that
f
has a global minimum at some point
x_{0}
and is strictly
decreasing for
x<x_{0}
and strictly increasing for
x>x_{0}
.
Since
f(0)
and
f(1)
are both negative, we obtain

f(x)<0
for all
x
in the interval
[0,1]
.
But this contradicts
f(y)\geqslant 0
.

This yields that at least one of the schedulers
\mathfrak{V}_{0}

or
\mathfrak{V}_{1}
witnesses the violation of (
GPR
).
Thus, we can define
\mathfrak{T}_{i}\in\{\mathfrak{V}_{0},\mathfrak{V}_{1}\}
accordingly.

The number of states
k
in
U
is bounded by the number of states in
S
. In each iteration of the above construction, the function value
f(0)
is sufficient to determine one of the schedulers
\mathfrak{V}_{0}

and
\mathfrak{V}_{1}
witnessing the violation of (
GPR
).
So, the procedure has to compute the values in condition (
GPR-1
) for
k
-many MR-schedulers and update the scheduler afterwards.
As the update can easily be carried out in polynomial time, the run-time of all
k
iterations is polynomial as well.

The condition that
\tau
only has to be scheduled with probability
0
or
1
in each state is the key to transfer the sufficiency of MR-schedulers to the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
. This fact is of general interest as well and stated in the following theorem where
\tau
again is the action added to move from a state
s_{\mathcal{E}}
to the new trap state in the MEC-quotient.

Theorem 25
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP with pairwise disjoint action sets for all states.
Then, for each MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{M}
with

\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)\in\{0,1\}
for each MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
there is an MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}
such that every action
\alpha
of
\mathcal{M}
that does not belong to an MEC of
\mathcal{M}
, has the same expected frequency under
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{T}
.

Proof 4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be an MR-scheduler for
\mathit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
with
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)\in\{0,1\}
for each MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
.
We consider the following extension
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
: The state space of
\mathcal{M}
is extended by a new terminal state
\bot
and
a fresh action
\tau
is enabled in each state
s
that belongs to a MEC of
\mathcal{M}
. Action
\tau
leads to
\bot
with probability
1
. All remaining transition probabilities are as in
\mathcal{M}
.
So,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{M}
by allowing a transition to a new terminal state
\bot
from each state that belongs to a MEC.

Now, we first provide a finite-memory scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
that leaves each MEC
\mathcal{E}
for which
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)=0
via the state action pair
(s,\alpha)
with probability
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\alpha)
. Recall that we assume that each action is enabled in at most one state and that the actions enabled in the state
s_{\mathcal{E}}
in
\mathit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
are precisely the actions that are enabled in some state of
\mathcal{E}
and that do not belong to
\mathcal{E}
(Section
2.3
).

Let us define the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
:
In all states that do not belong to a MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)=0
, the behavior of
\mathfrak{T}
is memoryless:
For each state
s
of
\mathcal{M}
(and hence of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
) that does not belong to a MEC,
\mathfrak{T}(s)=\mathfrak{S}(s)
. For each state
s
in an end component

\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)=1
, we define
\mathfrak{T}(s)(\tau)=1
.
If a MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)=0
is entered,
\mathfrak{T}
makes use of finitely many memory modes as follows:
Enumerate the state action pairs
(s,\alpha)
where
s
belongs to
\mathcal{E}
, but
\alpha
does not belong to
\mathcal{E}
, and for which
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\alpha)>0
by

(s_{1},\alpha_{1})
, …,
(s_{k},\alpha_{k})
.
Further, let
p_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\alpha_{i})>0
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
By assumption
\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}p_{i}=1
.
When entering
\mathcal{E}
, the scheduler works in
k
memory modes
1
, …,
k
until an action
\alpha
that does not belong to
\mathcal{E}
is scheduled
starting in memory mode
1
.
In each memory mode
i
,

\mathfrak{T}
follows an MD-scheduler for
\mathcal{E}
that reaches
s_{i}
with probability
1
from all states of
\mathcal{E}
.
Once,
s_{i}
is reached,
\mathfrak{T}
chooses action
\alpha_{i}
with probability

q_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\frac{p_{i}}{1-\sum_{j<i}p_{j}}.

Now
\mathfrak{T}
leaves
\mathcal{E}
via
(s_{k},\alpha_{k})
with probability
1
if it reaches the last memory mode
k
.
As
\mathfrak{T}
behaves MD in each mode, it leaves the end component
\mathcal{E}
after finitely many steps in expectation.
Furthermore, for each
i\leq k
, it leaves
\mathcal{E}
via
(s_{i},\alpha_{i})
with probability
(1-\sum_{j<i}p_{j})\cdot q_{i}=p_{i}
.
Since the behavior of
\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
is mimicked by
\mathfrak{T}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, we conclude that the expected frequency of actions in
\mathcal{M}
which do not belong to an end component is the same in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
under
\mathfrak{T}
and in
\mathit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
under
\mathfrak{S}
.

The expected frequency of each state-action pair of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
under
\mathfrak{T}
is finite, since each MEC of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is left after finitely many steps in expectation.
In the terminology of
[
Kal20
]
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
is
transient
.
By
[
Kal20
, Theorem 9.16]
, this implies that there is a MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{U}
for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
under which the expected frequency of state-action pairs is the same as under
\mathfrak{T}
and thus the expected frequency in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of actions
\alpha
of
\mathcal{M}
that do not belong to an end component is the same as under
\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
.

Finally, we modify
\mathfrak{U}
such that it becomes a scheduler for
\mathcal{M}
: For each end component
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathfrak{S}(s_{\mathcal{E}})(\tau)=1
, we fix a memoryless scheduler
\mathfrak{U}_{\mathcal{E}}
that does not leave the end component. Now, whenever a state
s
in such an end component is visited, the modified scheduler switches to the behavior of
\mathfrak{U}_{\mathcal{E}}
instead of choosing action
\tau
with probability
1
. Clearly, this does not affect the expected frequency of actions of
\mathcal{M}
that do not belong to an end component and hence the modified scheduler is as claimed in the theorem.

Remark 26
.

The proof of Theorem
25
above provides an algorithm how to obtain the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
from
\mathfrak{S}
. The number of memory modes of the intermediately constructed finite-memory scheduler is bounded by the number of state-action pairs of
\mathcal{M}
. Further, in each memory mode during the traversal of a MEC, the scheduler behaves in a memoryless deterministic way. Hence, the induced Markov chain is of size polynomial in the size of the MDP
\mathcal{M}
and the representation of the scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
. Therefore, also the expected frequencies of all state-action pairs under the intermediate finite-memory scheduler and hence under
\mathfrak{T}
can be computed in time polynomial in the size of the MDP
\mathcal{M}
and the representation of the scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
.
So, also the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
itself which can be derived from these expected frequencies can be computed in polynomial time from
\mathfrak{S}
.

Together with Lemma
24
, this means that
\mathfrak{T}
and hence the scheduler with two memory modes whose existence is stated in Theorem
18
can be computed from a solution to the constraint system (S1)-(S5) from Section
4.2
in time polynomial in the size of the original MDP and the size of the representation of the solution to (S1)-(S5).

\lhd

With these results we can now prove the second main result of this section, Theorem
18
, stating that if (
GPR
) does not hold there is a finite-memory scheduler with two memory cells refuting the GPR condition.

Proof 4.10

(Proof of Theorem
18
)
.

The model transformation establishing assumptions (A1)-(A3) results in the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
up to the renaming and collapsing of terminal states. By Corollary
23
and Theorem
25
, we conclude that

\mathsf{Cause}
is not a GPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if there is a MR-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
that violates (
GPR
).
As in Remark
15
,

\mathfrak{S}
can be extended to
a finite-memory randomized scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}
with two memory cells.

Remark 27

(On lower bounds on GPR checking)
.

Solving systems of quadratic inequalities with linear side constraints is NP-hard in general (see, e.g.,
[
GJ79
]
).
For convex problems, in which the associated symmetric matrix occurring in the quadratic inequality has only non-negative eigenvalues, the problem is, however, solvable in polynomial time
[
KTK80
]
.
Unfortunately, the quadratic constraint system describing a scheduler refuting (
GPR
) given by (S1)-(S5) is not of this form.
We observe that even if
\mathsf{Cause}
is a singleton
\{c\}
and the variable
x_{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}
is forced to take a constant value
y
by (S1)-(S3), i.e., by the structure of the MDP, the inequality (S4) takes the form:

\displaystyle x_{c}\cdot w_{c}-x_{c}^{2}\cdot(w_{c}+y)\leq 0

(7)

Here, the
1\times 1
-matrix
({-}w_{c}{-}y)
has a negative eigenvalue.
Although it is not ruled out that
(S1)-(S5) belongs to another class of
efficiently solvable constraint systems,
the NP-hardness result in
[
PV91
]

for the solvability of
quadratic inequalities of the form (
7
)
with linear side constraints
might be an indication for the computational difficulty.

\lhd

5.
Quality and optimality of causes

The goal of this section is to identify notions that measure how “good” causes are and to present algorithms to determine good causes according to the proposed quality measures.
We have seen so far that small (singleton) causes are easy to determine (see Section
4.1
).
Moreover, it is easy to see that the proposed existence-checking algorithm can be formulated in such a way that the algorithm returns a singleton (strict or global) probability-raising cause
\{c_{0}\}
with maximal
precision
, i.e., a state
c_{0}
where

\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}|\Diamond c_{0})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M},c_{0}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
is maximal.
On the other hand,
singleton or small cause sets might have poor coverage in the sense that the probability for paths that reach an effect state without visiting a cause state before (“uncovered effects”) can be large.
This motivates the consideration of quality notions for causes that incorporate how well
effect scenarios are covered.
We take inspiration of quality measures that are considered in statistical analysis (see e.g.
[
Pow11
]
).
This includes the
recall
as a measure for the relative coverage (proportion of covered effects among all effect scenarios), the
coverage ratio
(quotient of covered and uncovered effects) as well as the
f-score
.
The f-score is a standard measure for classifiers defined by the harmonic mean of precision and recall.
It can be seen as a compromise to achieve both good precision and good recall.

In this section, we assume as before an MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
and
\mathsf{Eff}\subseteq S
are given where all effect states are terminal.
Furthermore, we suppose all states
s\in S
are reachable from
\mathsf{init}
.

5.1.
Quality measures for causes

In statistical analysis, the precision of a classifier with binary outcomes (“positive” or “negative”) is defined as the ratio of all true positives among all positively classified elements, while its recall is defined as the ratio of all true positives among all actual positive elements.
Translated to our setting, we consider classifiers induced by a given cause set
\mathsf{Cause}
that return “positive” for sample paths in case that a cause state is visited
and “negative” otherwise.
The intuitive meaning of true positives, false positives, true negatives and false negatives is as described in the confusion matrix in Figure
13
.
The formal definition is

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}&=&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}),&\mathsf{tn}^{\mathfrak{S}}&=&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}),\\[4.30554pt]
\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}&=&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}),&\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}&=&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}).\end{array}

Path hits

\mathsf{Eff}

\neg\mathsf{Eff}

\mathsf{Cause}

True positive (tp)

False positive (fp)

\mathsf{Cause}
correctly predicted
\mathsf{Eff}

\mathsf{Cause}
falsely predicted
\mathsf{Eff}

\neg\mathsf{Cause}

False negative (fn)

True negative (tn)

\mathsf{Cause}
falsely not predicted
\mathsf{Eff}

\mathsf{Cause}
correctly not predicted
\mathsf{Eff}

Figure 13.
Confusion matrix for
\mathsf{Cause}
as a binary classifier for
\mathsf{Eff}

With this interpretation of causes as binary classifiers in mind, the recall and precision
and coverage ratio
of a cause set
\mathsf{Cause}

under a scheduler

\mathfrak{S}
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\ =&\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\ \Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\ )&=&\frac{\displaystyle\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\displaystyle\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}\\[8.61108pt]
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\ |\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )&=&\frac{\displaystyle\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\displaystyle\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}\\[8.61108pt]
\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\ =&\frac{\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\Diamond(\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\bigr)}{\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl((\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause})\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\bigr)}&=&\frac{\displaystyle\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\displaystyle\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}.\end{array}

Note that for these quality measures we make some respective assumptions on the scheduler as we assume

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
for
\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})
,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
for
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})
and

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl((\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause})\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\bigr)>0
) for
\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})
.

If we have
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl((\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause})\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\bigr)=0
and
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
for some scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
, we define
\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=+\infty
.
This makes sense since we can converge to such a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with a sequence of schedulers
\mathfrak{S}_{0}\ldots
for which
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}_{i}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl((\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause})\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\bigr)>0
and
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}_{i}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
The coverage ratio of such a sequence converges to
+\infty
.

Finally, the f-score of
\mathsf{Cause}

under a scheduler

\mathfrak{S}

is defined as the harmonic mean of the precision and recall.
Here we assume
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
, which implies

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
:

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ \ 2\cdot\frac{\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})\cdot\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}{\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})+\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}=\frac{2\cdot\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{2\cdot\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}

If, however,
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
and
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})=0
for some
\mathfrak{S}
, define
\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=0
.
This again makes sense as for a sequence of schedulers converging to
\mathfrak{S}
the f-score also converges to
0
(also see Lemma
32
).

To lift the definitions of the quality measures under a scheduler to the quality measure of a cause, we consider the worst-case scheduler:

{defi}
[Quality measures for causes]
Let
\mathsf{Cause}
be a PR cause.
We define

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ =\ \inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ =\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Cause}\ |\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )

when ranging over all schedulers
\mathfrak{S}

with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
.
Likewise, the coverage ratio and f-score of
\mathsf{Cause}

are defined by the worst-case coverage ratio resp. f-score
– ranging over schedulers for which
\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

resp.
\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})
is defined:

\displaystyle\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})\ =\ \inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause}),\quad\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\ =\ \inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause}).

Besides the quality measures defined so far, which we will address in detail, there is a vast landscape of further quality measures for binary classifiers in the literature (for an overview, see, e.g.,
[
Pow11
]
).
One prominent example which has been claimed to be superior to the f-score recently
[
CJ20
]
is
Matthews correlation coefficient
(MCC). In terms of the entries of a confusion matrix (as in Figure
13
), it is defined as

\displaystyle\text{MCC}=\frac{tp\cdot tn-fp\cdot fn}{\sqrt{(tp+fp)\cdot(tp+fn)\cdot(tn+fp)\cdot(tn+fn)}}

In contrast to the f-score (as well as recall and coverage ratio), it makes use of all four entries of the confusion matrix.
In our setting, we could assign the MCC to a
\mathsf{Cause}
by again taking the infimum of the value over all sensible schedulers.

Like the MCC, almost all (cf.
[
Pow11
]
) of the quality measures studied in the literature are algebraic functions (intuitively speaking, built from polynomials, fractions and root functions) in the entries of the confusion matrix.
At the end of this section, we will comment on the computational properties of finding good causes when quality is measured by the infimum over all sensible schedulers of an algebraic function in the entries of the confusion matrix.

5.2.
Computation schemes for the quality measures for fixed cause set

For this section, we assume a fixed PR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
is given and address the problem to compute its quality values.
The first observation is, that all quality measures are preserved by the switch from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
as well as the transformations of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
to an MDP that satisfies conditions (A1)-(A3) of Section
4.2
.
In the following Lemmata
28
and
29
we show that the quality measures
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}},\mathit{covrat}
and
\mathit{fscore}
of a fixed
\mathsf{Cause}
are compatible with the model transformations from Section 4.
These are, on one hand a transformation to
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
, which only considers the minimal probability to reach
\mathsf{Eff}
starting from
\mathsf{Cause}
, and on the other hand a transformation to an MDP
\mathcal{N}
satisfying (A1)-(A3), which has no end components and has exactly four terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}},\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}},\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
.
These four terminal states exactly correspond to the four entries of the confusion matrix (Figure
13
).

Lemma 28
.

If
\mathsf{Cause}
is an SPR or a GPR cause then:

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

Proof 5.1
.

“
\leqslant
”:
A scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
can be seen as
a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
behaving as an MD-scheduler minimizing the reachability probability of
\mathsf{Eff}
from every state in
\mathsf{Cause}
and we have:

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

and therefore:

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

We obtain the claimed inequalities, e.g.

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})\leqslant\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})
.

“
\geqslant
”:
Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler of

\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be
the scheduler of
\mathcal{M}
that behaves as
\mathfrak{S}
until the first
visit to a state in
\mathsf{Cause}
. As soon as
\mathfrak{T}
has reached
\mathsf{Cause}
, it
behaves as an MD-scheduler minimizing the probability to reach
\mathsf{Eff}
.
Recall and coverage
under
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{S}
have the form:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\ =&\frac{x}{x+q}&&\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\ =&\frac{x}{q}\\
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\frac{y}{y+q}&&\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\frac{y}{q}\end{array}

where
x\geqslant y
(and
q=\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}
).
Considering
\mathfrak{T}
as a scheduler of
\mathcal{M}
and of

\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
, we get:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\geqslant&\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})\\
\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\geqslant&\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})\end{array}

This implies:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ \geqslant\ \operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause}),

and

\displaystyle\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ \geqslant\ \mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

With similar arguments we get:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})&\geqslant&\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})&=&\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})\end{array}

As the harmonic mean viewed as a function

f:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
f(x,y)=2\frac{xy}{x{+}y}

is monotonically increasing in both arguments
(note that
\frac{df}{dx}=\frac{y^{2}}{(x{+}y)^{2}}>0
and

\frac{df}{dy}=\frac{x^{2}}{(x{+}y)^{2}}>0
),
we obtain:

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ \geqslant\ \mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})\ =\ \mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}^{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathsf{Cause})

This yields

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{Cause})\geqslant\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})
.

Lemma 29
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}
for some MDP
\mathcal{M}
with a set of terminal states
\mathsf{Eff}
and
an SPR or a GPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
. Then:

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{covrat}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\mathsf{Cause})

=

\mathit{fscore}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathsf{Cause})

Proof 5.2
.

Analogously to the proof of Lemma
19
.

This now allows us to work under assumptions (A1)-(A3) when addressing problems concerning the quality measures for a fixed cause set.

As efficient computation methods for
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})

are known from literature (see
[
BKKM14
,
Mär20
]

for poly-time algorithms to compute
conditional reachability probabilities), we can use the same methods to compute the coverage ratio.

Corollary 30
.

The value
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})
and corresponding worst-case schedulers are computable in polynomial time.

Proof 5.3
.

For a given scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
we have

\displaystyle\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}

We thus get the following

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}=\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}+\frac{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}=1+\frac{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}=1+\frac{1}{\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}}.

This implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}=1+\frac{1}{\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}\ \text{thus}\ \mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=1/\left(\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}-1\right).

Computing
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})
now implores us to take the infimum of all sensible schedulers over
1/\left(\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}-1\right)
which is the same as taking the infimum of all sensible schedulers over
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})
.
This amounts to computing

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\min}(\ \lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\mid\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\ ),

which can be computed in polynomial time by
[
BKKM14
,
Mär20
]
.

In contrast to these results, we are not aware of
known concepts that are applicable
for computing the f-score.
Indeed, this quality measure is efficiently computable:

Theorem 31
.

The value
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
and corresponding worst-case schedulers are computable in polynomial time.

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
31
.
We can express
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
in terms of the supremum of a quotient of reachability probabilities for disjoint sets of terminal states.
More precisely, under assumptions (A1)-(A3) and assuming
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})>0
,
we have:

\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=\frac{2}{X+2}\quad\text{where}\quad X\ =\ \sup_{\mathfrak{S}}\,\frac{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})+\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})}{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})}

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})>0
.
Moreover, we can show that we can handle the corner case of
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=0
.

Lemma 32
.

Let
\mathsf{Cause}
be an SPR or a GPR cause. Then, the following three statements
are equivalent:

(a)

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})=0

(b)

\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=0

(c)

There is a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
such that

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})=0
.

Proof 5.4
.

Let
C=\mathsf{Cause}
.
Using results of
[
BKKM14
,
Mär20
]
, there exist schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{U}

with

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0

and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond C\ |\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )=\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond C\ |\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with positive
effect probability,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0

and

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\Diamond C\ )=\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\Diamond C\ )

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0
.

In particular,

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond C\ |\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )

and

\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}(C)=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{U}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\Diamond C\ )
.
By (
GPR
) applied to
\mathfrak{U}
and
\mathfrak{T}

(recall that each SPR cause is a GPR cause too,
see Lemma
5
),
we obtain the following statements (i) and (ii):

(i)
:

p\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ \operatorname{\mathit{precision}}(C)\ >\ 0

(ii)
:

If
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0

then
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0

and therefore

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)>0
.

Obviously,
if there is no scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
as in statement (c) then

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0
.
Thus, from (ii) we get:

(iii)
:

If there is no scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
as in statement (c)
then

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)>0
.

“(a)
\Longrightarrow
(b)”:
We prove
\mathit{fscore}(C)>0
implies
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)>0
.
If
\mathit{fscore}(C)>0
then, by definition of the f-score, there is no scheduler

\mathfrak{S}
as in statement (c).
But then
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)>0
by statement (iii).

“(b)
\Longrightarrow
(c)”:
Let
\mathit{fscore}(C)=0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there is no scheduler as in (c).
Again by (iii) we obtain
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)>0
.
But then, for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0
:

\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\ \geqslant\ p\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny(i)}}}{{>}}\ 0

and, with
r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)
:

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\ \geqslant\ r\ >\ 0

The harmonic mean as a function

]0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
(x,y)\mapsto 2\frac{xy}{x+y}
is
monotonically increasing in both arguments.
But then:

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\ \geqslant\ 2\frac{p\cdot r}{p{+}r}\ >\ 0

Hence,
\mathit{fscore}(C)=\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\geqslant 2\frac{p\cdot r}{p{+}r}>0
.
Contradiction.

“(c)
\Longrightarrow
(a)”:
Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler as in statement (c).
Then,

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond C\ |\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )\ =\ 0.

Hence:

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)\ =\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond C\ |\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )\ =\ 0
.

The remaining task to prove Theorem
31
is a generally applicable technique for computing extremal ratios of reachability probabilities in MDPs without ECs.

Max/min ratios of reachability probabilities for disjoint sets of terminal states.

Suppose we are given an MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
without ECs and disjoint subsets
U,V\subseteq S
of terminal states.
Given a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond V)>0
we define:

\displaystyle\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\ =\ \frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond U)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond V)}

The goal is an algorithm for computing the extremal values:

\displaystyle\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}=\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\quad\text{and}\quad\ratio{\max}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}=\sup_{\mathfrak{S}}\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond V)>0
.

To compute these, we rely on a polynomial reduction to the classical
stochastic shortest path problem

[
BT91
]
.
For this, consider the MDP
\mathcal{N}
arising from
\mathcal{M}
by adding reset transitions from all terminal states
t\in S\backslash V
to
\mathsf{init}
.
Thus, exactly the
V
-states are terminal in
\mathcal{N}
.

\mathcal{N}
might contain ECs, which, however, do not intersect with
V
.
We equip
\mathcal{N}
with the weight function that assigns
1
to all states in
U
and
0
to all other states.
For a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond V)=1
, let
\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
be the expected accumulated weight until reaching
V
under
\mathfrak{T}
.
Let
\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)=\inf_{\mathfrak{T}}\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
and
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)=\sup_{\mathfrak{T}}\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
,
where
\mathfrak{T}
ranges over all schedulers
with

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond V)=1
.

We can rely on known results
[
BT91
,
dA99
,
BBD+18
]
to obtain
that both

\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
and
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)

are computable in polynomial time.
As
\mathcal{N}
has only non-negative weights,

\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
is finite
and a corresponding MD-scheduler with minimal expectation exists.
If
\mathcal{N}
has an EC containing at least one
U
-state,
which is the case iff
\mathcal{M}
has a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}

with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond U)>0
and
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond V)=0
,
then

\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)=+\infty
.
Otherwise,
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)

is finite and the maximum is achieved by an MD-scheduler as well.

Theorem 33
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP without ECs and

U,V
disjoint sets of terminal states in
\mathcal{M}
, and let
\mathcal{N}
be as before.
Then,

\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}=\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
and

\ratio{\max}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}=\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
.
Thus, both values are computable in polynomial time,
and there is an MD-scheduler minimizing
\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
,
and an MD-scheduler maximizing
\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
if

\ratio{\max}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
is finite.

\mathsf{init}
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
c
\mathsf{noeff}
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
1/2
1/2
1/2
1/2
1/2
\alpha
\beta
1/2

Figure 14.
MDP
\mathcal{M}
from Example
5.2

\mathsf{init}
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
c
\mathsf{noeff}
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
1/2
1/2
1/2
1/2
1/2
\alpha
\beta
1/2

Figure 15.
MDP
\mathcal{N}
with reset transitions for
\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}

{exa}

Consider the MDP
\mathcal{M}
from Figure
15
with
\mathsf{Eff}=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}\}
and suppose the task is to compute
\mathit{covrat}(c)=\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}
.
The construction for the algorithm is depicted in Figure
15
resulting in
\mathcal{N}
, where reset transitions for
\mathsf{noeff}
and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
have been added (red edges) and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
is the only terminal state.
The weight function now assigns
1
to
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
0
to all others.
By Theorem
33
we have
\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}}=\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}).

\lhd

Proof 5.5

(Proof of Theorem
33
)
.

\mathcal{M}
has a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}

with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond U)>0
and
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond V)=0

if and only if
the transformed MDP
\mathcal{N}
in Section
5.2

(Max/min ratios of reachability probabilities for disjoint sets of terminal states)
has an EC containing at least one
U
-state.
Therefore, we then have

\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)=+\infty.

Otherwise,
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
is finite.

For the following we only consider
\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}=\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
since the arguments for the maximum are similar.
First, we show

\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\geqslant\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
.
For this, we consider an arbitrary scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
.
Let

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond U)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond V)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=1-x-p

For
p>0
we have

\frac{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond U)}{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond V)}\ \ =\ \ \frac{x}{p}

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be the scheduler that behaves as
\mathfrak{S}

in the first round and after each reset.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\ \ =\ \ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}n\cdot x^{n}\cdot\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)q^{k}\cdot p\ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\eqref{second basic}}}}{{=}}\ \ \frac{x}{p}

where (
34
) relies on some basic calculations
(see Lemma
34
).
This yields:

\ratio{\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\ =\ \frac{x}{p}\ =\ \mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\ \geqslant\ \mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)

Hence,

\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\geqslant\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
.

For the other direction
\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\geqslant\ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
, we use the fact that there is an MD-scheduler

\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{N}
such that

\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)=\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
.

\mathfrak{T}
can be viewed as an MD-scheduler for the original MDP
\mathcal{M}
.
Again we can rely on (
5.5
) to obtain that:

\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\ \ =\ \ \frac{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\Diamond U\bigr)}{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\Diamond V\bigr)}\ \ =\ \ \ratio{\mathfrak{T}}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}\ \ \geqslant\ \ \ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}

But this yields

\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\ \geqslant\ \ratio{\min}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
.
For
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)\ =\ \ratio{\max}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
we use similar arguments.
