We can now rely on known results
[
BT91
,
dA99
,
BBD+18
]
to compute
\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
and
\mathrm{E}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\boxplus V)
in polynomial time.

Lemma 34
.

Let

x,p,q\in[0,1]
such that
x{+}q{+}p=1
.
Then:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}n\cdot x^{n}\cdot\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)q^{k}\cdot p\ \ =\ \ \frac{x}{p}

Proof 5.6
.

We first show for
0<q<1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)q^{k},

we have

a_{n}=\frac{1}{(1{-}q)^{n+1}}

This is done by induction on
n
. The claim is clear for
n{=}0
.
For the step of induction we use:

\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}1{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\ \ =\ \ \left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\ +\ \left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k{-}1\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\ \ =\ \ \left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\ +\ \left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}(n{+}1)+(k{-}1)\\
k{-}1\end{array}\!\!\!\right)

But this yields
a_{n+1}=a_{n}+q\cdot a_{n+1}
.
Hence:

a_{n+1}\ =\ \frac{a_{n}}{1{-}q}

The claim then follows directly from the induction hypothesis.

The statement of Lemma
34
now follows
by some calculations and
the preliminary induction.

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}n\cdot x^{n}\cdot\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n{+}k\\
k\end{array}\!\!\!\right)q^{k}\cdot p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\cdot x^{n}\cdot\frac{1}{(1{-}q)^{n+1}}\cdot p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p}{1{-}q}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\cdot\left(\frac{x}{1{-}q}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p}{1{-}q}\cdot\frac{\displaystyle\frac{x}{1{-}q}}{\displaystyle\ \Bigl(1-\frac{x}{1{-}q}\Bigr)^{2}\ }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{px}{(1{-}q{-}x)^{2}}\ \ \ =\ \ \ \frac{px}{p^{2}}\ \ \ =\ \ \ \frac{x}{p}

where we use
p=1{-}q{-}x
.

Applying this framework for
\ratio{\max}{\mathcal{M}}{U,V}
to the f-score we now prove Theorem
31
.

Proof 5.7

(Proof of Theorem
31
)
.

We use the simplifying assumptions (A1)-(A3) that can be made due to Lemmas
28
and
29
.
For
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
we have
after some straight-forward transformations

\displaystyle\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}.

Using this we get

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}-2

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}=\frac{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})+\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})}{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})}

Thus, the task is to compute

X=\sup_{\mathfrak{S}}\frac{2}{\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{Cause})}-2=\sup_{\mathfrak{S}}\frac{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})+\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})}{\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})},

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\Diamond\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})>0
.
We have

\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=\frac{2}{X+2}.

But
X
can be expressed as a supremum in the form of Theorem
33
.
This yields the claim that the optimal value is computable in polynomial time.

In case
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=0
, we do not obtain an optimal scheduler via Theorem
33
.
Lemma
32
, however, shows that there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
and
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})=0
.
Such a scheduler can be computed in polynomial time as any (memoryless) scheduler in the largest sub-MDP of
\mathcal{M}
that does not contain states in
\mathsf{Cause}
.
(This sub-MDP can be constructed by successively removing states and state-action pairs.)

5.3.
Quality-optimal probability-raising causes

For the computation there is no difference between GPR and SPR causes as only the quality properties of the set are in question.
However, when finding optimal causes the distinction makes a difference.
Here, we say an SPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
is
recall-optimal
if
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})=\max_{C}\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)
where
C
ranges over all SPR causes.
Likewise,
ratio-optimality
resp.
f-score-optimality
of
\mathsf{Cause}
means maximality of
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})
resp.
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
among all SPR causes.
Recall-, ratio- and f-score-optimality for GPR causes are defined accordingly.

Lemma 35
.

Let
\mathsf{Cause}
be an SPR or a GPR cause.
Then,
\mathsf{Cause}
is recall-optimal if and only if
\mathsf{Cause}
is ratio-optimal.

Proof 5.8
.

Essentially the proof uses the same connection between
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}
and
\mathit{covrat}
as Corollary
30
.
Here we do not assume (A1)-(A3).
However, for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
and each set
C
of states we have:

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ =\ \mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}+\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}

where

\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl((\neg\lozenge C)\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\bigr)

and

\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\Diamond(C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\bigr)
.
If
C
is a cause where
\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}
is positive then
\mathit{covrat}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\ =\ \frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}

and

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)\ =\ \frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}+\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}
.

For all non-negative reals
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
where
q,q^{\prime}>0
we have:

\frac{p}{q}<\frac{p^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\frac{p}{p+q}<\frac{p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}.

Hence, if
C
is fixed and
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers with

\mathsf{tp}_{C}^{\mathfrak{S}}>0
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}\ \text{ is minimal iff }\ \frac{\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}+\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{C}}\ \text{ is minimal}

Thus, if
C
is fixed and
\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{S}_{C}
is a scheduler achieving the
worst-case (i.e., minimal) coverage ratio for
C
then

\mathfrak{S}
achieves the minimal recall for
C
, and vice versa.

Let now
\mathsf{fn}_{C}=\mathsf{fn}_{C}^{\mathfrak{S}_{C}}
,
\mathsf{tp}_{C}=\mathsf{tp}_{C}^{\mathfrak{S}_{C}}
where

\mathfrak{S}_{C}
is a scheduler that minimizes the coverage ratio and
minimizes the recall for cause set
C
.
Then:

\mathit{covrat}(C)=\frac{\mathsf{tp}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}_{C}}
is maximal iff
\frac{\mathsf{tp}_{C}}{\mathsf{fn}_{C}+\mathsf{tp}_{C}}
is maximal iff
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C)
is maximal

where the extrema range over all SPR resp. GPR causes
C
.
This yields the claim.

Recall- and ratio-optimal SPR causes.

The techniques of Section
4.1

yield an algorithm for generating a canonical SPR
cause with optimal recall and coverage ratio.
To see this, let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of all states which constitute a singleton SPR cause.
The canonical cause
\mathsf{CanCause}
is defined as the set of states
c\in\mathcal{C}
such that there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}((\neg\mathcal{C})\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
.
So to speak
\mathsf{CanCause}
is the “front” of
\mathcal{C}
.
Obviously,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathsf{CanCause}
are computable in polynomial time.

Theorem 36
.

If
\mathcal{C}\not=\varnothing
then
\mathsf{CanCause}
is a ratio- and recall-optimal SPR cause.

Proof 5.9
.

By definition of SPR causes any subset
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
satisfying
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}(\neg C\,\mathrm{U}\,c)
for each
c\in C
constitutes an SPR cause and thus
\mathsf{CanCause}
is also an SPR cause.
Optimality is a consequence as
\mathsf{CanCause}
even yields
path-wise optimal coverage in the following sense.
If
C
is any SPR cause then
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
by definition and for each path
\pi
in
\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\pi\models(\neg\lozenge\mathsf{CanCause})\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}

\displaystyle\implies\pi\models(\neg\lozenge C)\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\pi\models\Diamond(C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})

\displaystyle\implies\pi\models\Diamond(\mathsf{CanCause}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}).

But then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(\mathsf{CanCause}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\lozenge C)\wedge\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}))

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\lozenge\mathsf{CanCause})\wedge\mathsf{Eff})

for every scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
,which yields the claim.

\mathsf{init}
\mathsf{eff}
\mathsf{noeff}
s_{1}
s_{2}
1/4
1/4
1/2
1/4
3/4
1

Figure 16.
Markov chain from Remark
37

Remark 37
.

It is not true that the canonical SPR cause
\mathsf{CanCause}
is f-score-optimal.
To see this, Consider the Markov chain from Figure
16
.
There we have
\mathsf{CanCause}=\{s_{1}\}
, which has
\operatorname{\mathit{precision}}(\mathsf{CanCause})=\frac{3}{4}
and
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{CanCause})=\frac{3}{8}/(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{8})=\frac{3}{5}.

But the SPR cause
\{s_{2}\}
has better f-score as its precision is
1
and it has the same recall as
\mathsf{CanCause}
.

\lhd

F-score-optimal SPR cause.

From Section
5.2
, we see that f-score-optimal SPR causes in MDPs can be computed
in polynomial space by computing the f-score for all potential SPR causes one by one in polynomial time (Theorem
31
).
As the space can be reused after each computation, this results in polynomial space.
For Markov chains, we can do better
and compute an f-score-optimal SPR cause in polynomial time
via a polynomial reduction to the stochastic shortest path problem:

Theorem 38
.

In Markov chains that have SPR causes, an f-score-optimal SPR cause can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof 5.10
.

We regard the given Markov chain

\mathcal{M}
as an MDP with a singleton action set
\mathit{Act}=\{\alpha\}
.
As
\mathcal{M}
has SPR causes, the set
\mathcal{C}

of states that constitute a singleton SPR cause
is nonempty.
We may assume that
\mathcal{M}
has no non-trivial (i.e., cyclic)
bottom strongly connected components as we may collapse them.
Let
w_{c}

=

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M},c}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
.

We switch from
\mathcal{M}
to a new MDP
\mathcal{K}
with state space

S_{\mathcal{K}}=S\cup\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}\}
with
fresh states
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}

and the action set

\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}=\{\alpha,\gamma\}
.
The MDP
\mathcal{K}
arises from
\mathcal{M}

by adding

(i):

for each SPR state
c\in\mathcal{C}
a fresh state-action pair
(c,\gamma)
such that
P_{\mathcal{K}}(c,\gamma,\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})=w_{c}
and
P_{\mathcal{K}}(c,\gamma,\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}})=1{-}w_{c}
and

(ii):

reset transitions to
\mathsf{init}
with action label
\alpha

from the new state
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
and
all terminal states of
\mathcal{M}
,
i.e.,

P_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}},\alpha,\mathsf{init})=1
and
P_{\mathcal{K}}(s,\alpha,\mathsf{init})=1

for
s\in\mathsf{Eff}
or if
s
is a terminal
non-effect state of
\mathcal{M}
.

So, exactly
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
is terminal in
\mathcal{K}
,
and
\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(c)=\{\alpha,\gamma\}
for
c\in\mathcal{C}
, while

\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)=\{\alpha\}

for all other states
s
.
Intuitively, taking action
\gamma
in state
c\in\mathcal{C}
selects

c
to be a cause state.
The states in
\mathsf{Eff}
represent uncovered effects in
\mathcal{K}
,
while
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
stands for covered effects.

We assign weight
1
to all states
in
U=\mathsf{Eff}\cup\{\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}\}

and weight
0
to all other states of
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
V=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}\}
.
Then,
f=\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boxplus V)

and an MD-scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{K}
such that

\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boxplus V)=f
are computable in
polynomial time.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}

denote the set of states
c\in\mathcal{C}
where

\mathfrak{S}(c)=\gamma

and let
\mathsf{Cause}
be the set of states
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}

where
\mathcal{M}
has a path satisfying

(\neg\mathcal{C}_{\gamma})\,\mathrm{U}\,c
.
Then,
\mathsf{Cause}
is an SPR cause of
\mathcal{M}
.
With arguments as in Section
5.2

we obtain

\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})=2/(f{+}2)
.

It remains to show that
\mathsf{Cause}
is f-score-optimal.
Let
C
be an arbitrary SPR cause.
Then,
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be the MD-scheduler
for
\mathcal{K}
that schedules
\gamma
in
C
and

\alpha
for all other states of
\mathcal{K}
.
Then,
\mathit{fscore}(C)=2/(f^{\mathfrak{T}}{+}2)

where
f^{\mathfrak{T}}=\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boxplus V)
.
Hence,
f\leqslant f^{\mathfrak{T}}
, which yields

\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\geqslant\mathit{fscore}(C)
.

The naïve adaption of
the construction presented in the proof of Theorem
38

for MDPs would yield a stochastic game structure where the objective of
one player is to minimize the expected accumulated weight until reaching
a target state.
Although algorithms for
stochastic shortest path (SSP) games
are known

[
PB99
]
,
they rely on assumptions on the game structure which would not
be satisfied here.
However, for the threshold problem
SPR-f-score
where inputs are an MDP
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathsf{Eff}
and
\vartheta\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and the task is to decide the existence of an SPR cause whose f-score exceeds
\vartheta
, we can establish a polynomial reduction to SSP games, which yields an
\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}
upper bound:

Theorem 39
.

The decision problem SPR-f-score is in
\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}
.

Recall that for a given SPR cause
C
and scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
we have

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)>\vartheta
iff
\frac{\displaystyle 2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\displaystyle 2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}>\vartheta
.

In order to proof the upper bound of SPR-f-score we reformulate the condition of SPR-f-score.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
be an MDP with a set of terminal states
\mathsf{Eff}
, let
C
be an SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
, and let
\vartheta
be a rational.
Then,
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
iff

\displaystyle 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}>0

(14)

for all schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
.

Proof 5.11
.

Assume that
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
and let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
.
If
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)=0
, then
\mathit{fscore}(C)
would be
0
. So,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C)>0
.
Then,

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)=\frac{\displaystyle 2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}}{\displaystyle 2\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}+\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}}>\vartheta,\ \ \
which implies
\ \ \ 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}>0

Now, suppose that (
14
) holds for a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
. Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler that minimizes

\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)
. Such a scheduler exists by Theorem
31
.
From (
14
), we conclude

2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}>0

and hence that
\mathit{fscore}^{\mathfrak{S}}(C)>\vartheta
as above.

Proof 5.12

(Proof of Theorem
39
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
be an MDP,
\mathsf{Eff}\subseteq S
a set of terminal states, and
\vartheta
a rational.
Consider
\mathcal{C}
, the set of states
c\in S\setminus\mathsf{Eff}
where
\{c\}
is an SPR cause.
If
\mathcal{C}
is empty then the threshold problem is trivial as there is no SPR cause at all.
Thus, we suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is nonempty.

Note that
\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M},c}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
As the states in
\mathsf{Eff}
are not part of any end component of
\mathcal{M}
, no state
c\in\mathcal{C}
is contained in an end component of
\mathcal{M}
either.
Let
\mathcal{N}=(S_{\mathcal{N}},\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}},P_{\mathcal{N}},\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}})
be the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{M}
with the new additional terminal state
\bot
. The MEC-quotient
\mathcal{N}
contains the states from
\mathsf{Eff}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

Claim 1:

There is an SPR cause
C
for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
if and only if there is an SPR cause
C^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathit{fscore}(C^{\prime})>\vartheta
.

Proof of Claim 1.
We first observe that all reachability probabilities involved in the claim do not depend on the behavior during the traversal of MECs. Furthermore, staying inside a MEC in
\mathcal{M}
can be mimicked in
\mathcal{N}
by moving to
\bot
, and vice versa.
More precisely, let
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. Then,
analogously to Lemma
19
, for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{N}
, and vice versa,
such that

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\mid(\neg C)\,\mathrm{U}\,c)=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\mid(\neg C)\,\mathrm{U}\,c)
for all
c\in C
where the values are defined,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\mid\Diamond C)=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\mid\Diamond C)
if the values are defined, and

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C\mid\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond C\mid\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
if the values are defined.

Hence,
C
is an SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if it is in
\mathcal{N}
and furthermore, if it is an SPR cause, the f-score of
C
in
\mathcal{M}
and in
\mathcal{N}
agree. This finishes the proof of Claim 1.

Model transformation
for ensuring positive effect probabilities.

Recall that the f-score is only defined for schedulers reaching
\mathsf{Eff}
with positive probability.
Now, we will provide a further model transformation that will ensure that
\mathsf{Eff}
is reached with positive probability under all schedulers.
If this is already the case, there is nothing to do. So, we assume now that
\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N},\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=0
.

We define the subset of states from which
\mathsf{Eff}
can be avoided as
D\subseteq S_{\mathcal{N}}
by

D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{s\in S_{\mathcal{N}}\mid\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{N},s}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=0\}.

Note that
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}}\in D
.
For each
s\in D
, we further define the set of actions minimizing the reachability probability of
\mathsf{Eff}
from
s
by

\mathit{Act}^{\min}(s)=\{\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}}(s)\mid P_{\mathcal{N}}(s,\alpha,D)=1\}.

Finally, let
E\subseteq D
be the set of states that are reachable from
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}}
when only choosing actions from
\mathit{Act}^{\min}(\cdot)
. Note that
E
does not contain any states from
\mathcal{C}
, meaning no state in
E
constitutes a singleton SPR cause.

All schedulers that reach
\mathsf{Eff}
with positive probability in
\mathcal{N}
have to leave the sub-MDP consisting of
E
and the actions in
\mathit{Act}^{\min}(\cdot)
at some point. Let us call this sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
.
We define the set of state-action pairs
\Pi
that leave the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
:

\Pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{(s,\alpha)\mid s\in E\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}}(s)\setminus\mathit{Act}^{\min}(s)\}.

We now construct a further MDP
\mathcal{K}
. The idea is that
\mathcal{K}
behaves like the end-component free MDP
\mathcal{N}
after initially a scheduler is forced to choose a probability distribution over
state-action pairs from
\Pi
. In this way,
\mathsf{Eff}
is reached with positive probability under all schedulers.
Given an SPR cause, we will observe that for the f-score of this cause under a scheduler, it is only important how large the probabilities with which state action pairs from
\Pi
are chosen are relative to each other while the absolute values are not important. Due to this observation, for each SPR cause
C
and for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{N}
that reaches
\mathsf{Eff}
with positive probability, we can then construct a scheduler for
\mathcal{K}
that leads to the same recall and precision of
C
.

Formally,
\mathcal{K}
is defined as follows: The state space is
S_{\mathcal{N}}\cup\{\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}\}
where
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
is a fresh initial state.
For all states in
S_{\mathcal{N}}
, the same actions as in
\mathcal{N}
are available with the same transition probabilities. I.e., for all
s,t\in S_{\mathcal{N}}
,

\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}}(s)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\mathcal{K}}(s,\alpha,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}P_{\mathcal{N}}(s,\alpha,t)\text{ for all }\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s).

For each state-action pair
(s,\alpha)
from
\Pi
, we now add a new action
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
that is enabled only in
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
.
These are all actions enabled in
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
, i.e.,

\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{\beta_{(s,\alpha)}\mid(s,\alpha)\in\Pi\}.

For each state
t\in S_{\mathcal{N}}
, we define the transition probabilities under
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
by

P_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}},\beta_{(s,\alpha)},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}P_{\mathcal{N}}(s,\alpha,t).

Claim 2:

A subset
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
such that for all
c\in C:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{N}}(\neg C\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
is an SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta

if and only if
for all schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
, we have

\displaystyle 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}>0.

(15)

Proof of Claim 2.
We first prove the direction “
\Rightarrow
”. So, let
C
be an SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in end-component free MDP
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
.
As first observation we have that in order to prove (
15
) for all schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
, it suffices to consider schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
that start with a deterministic choice for state
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
and then behave in an arbitrary way.

To see this, we consider the MDP
\mathcal{K}_{C}
which consists of two copies of
\mathcal{K}
: “before
C
” and “after
C
”.
When
\mathcal{K}_{C}
enters a
C
-state in the first copy (“before
C
”), it switches to the second copy (“after
C
”) and stays there forever.
Let us write
(s,1)
for state
s
in the first copy and
(s,2)
for the copy of state
s
in the second copy.
Thus, in
\mathcal{K}_{C}
the event
\Diamond C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}
is equivalent to reaching a state
(\mathsf{eff},2)
where
\mathsf{eff}\in\mathsf{Eff}
, while
\Diamond C\wedge\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}
is equivalent to reaching a non-terminal state in the second copy, while
\neg\Diamond C\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}
corresponds to the event reaching an effect state in the first copy.

Obviously, there is a one-to-one-correspondence of the schedulers of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{C}
.
As
\mathcal{K}
has no end components so does
\mathcal{K}_{C}
.
Therefore, a terminal state will be reached almost surely under every scheduler.
Furthermore, we equip
\mathcal{K}_{C}
with a weight function on states which assigns

–

weight
2(1{-}\vartheta)
to the states
(\mathsf{eff},2)
where
\mathsf{eff}\in\mathsf{Eff}
,

–

weight
-\vartheta
to the states
(\mathsf{eff},1)
where
\mathsf{eff}\in\mathsf{Eff}
and
to the states
(s,2)
where
s
is a terminal non-effect state in
\mathcal{K}

(and
\mathcal{K}_{C}
), and

–

weight 0 to all other states.

Let
V
denote the set of all terminal states in
\mathcal{K}_{C}
.
Then, the expression on the left hand side of (
15
) equals
\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}_{C}}(\boxplus V)
, the expected accumulated weight until reaching a terminal state under scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
.
Hence, (
15
) holds for all schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
in
\mathcal{K}
if and only if
\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{K}_{C}}(\boxplus V)>0
.

It is well-known that the minimal expected accumulated weight in EC-free MDPs is achieved by an MD-scheduler
[
BK08
]
.
Thus, there is an MD-scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
of
\mathcal{K}_{C}
with
\mathrm{E}^{\min}_{\mathcal{K}_{C}}(\boxplus V)=\mathrm{E}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}_{C}}(\boxplus V)
.
When viewed as a scheduler of
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathfrak{T}
behaves memoryless deterministic before reaching
C
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{T}
’s initial choice in
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
is deterministic.

Recall the set
\mathit{Act}^{\min}(s)
of actions minimizing the reachability probability of
\mathsf{Eff}
from
s
.
Consider a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
with a deterministic choice
\mathfrak{T}(\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}})(\beta_{(s,\alpha)})=1
where
(s,\alpha)\in\Pi
.
To construct an analogous scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathcal{N}
, we pick an MD-scheduler
\mathfrak{U}
of the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
of
\mathcal{N}
induced by the state-action pairs
(u,\beta)
where
u\in E
and
\beta\in\mathit{Act}^{\min}(u)
such that there is a
\mathfrak{U}
-path from
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}}
to state
s
.

Scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathcal{N}
operates with the mode
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and the modes
\mathfrak{m}_{2,t}
for
t\in S_{\mathcal{N}}
.
In its initial mode
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
, scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
behaves as
\mathfrak{U}
as long as state
s
has not been visited.
When having reached state
s
in mode
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
, then
\mathfrak{S}
schedules the action
\alpha
with probability 1.
Let
t\in S_{\mathcal{N}}
be the state that
\mathfrak{S}
reaches via the
\alpha
-transition from
s
.
Then,
\mathfrak{S}
switches to mode
\mathfrak{m}_{2,t}
and behaves from then on as the residual scheduler
\mathit{res}(\mathfrak{T},\varpi)
of
\mathfrak{T}
for the
\mathfrak{T}
-path
\varpi=\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}\,\beta_{(s,\alpha)}\,t
in
\mathcal{K}
.
That is, after having scheduled the action
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
, scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
behaves exactly as
\mathfrak{T}
.

Let
\lambda
denote
\mathfrak{S}
’s probability to leave mode
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
, which equals
\mathfrak{U}
’s probability to reach
s
from
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{N}}
.
Thus,
\lambda=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\Diamond s)
when
\mathfrak{U}
is viewed as a scheduler of
\mathcal{N}
.
As
E
is disjoint from
C
and
\mathsf{Eff}
, scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
stays forever in mode
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and never reaches a state in
C\cup\mathsf{Eff}
with probability
1{-}\lambda
.

\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{T}
behave identically after choosing the state-action pair
(s,\alpha)\in\Pi
or the corresponding action
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
, respectively, which implies that

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond C\land\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Diamond C\land\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
,

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
and

•

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond C\land\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Diamond C\land\neg\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})
.

Recall the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
consisting of
E
and the actions in
\mathit{Act}^{\min}(\cdot)
.
As
\mathfrak{S}
leaves the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
with probability
\lambda>0
, we have
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
.
By Lemma
40
, we can conclude that

2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}>0.

By the equations above, this in turn implies that

2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}>0.

For the direction “
\Leftarrow
”, first recall that any subset of
\mathcal{C}
satisfying (M) is an SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
in
\mathcal{N}
by definition of
\mathcal{C}
. Now, let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})>0
.
Let
\Gamma
be the set of finite
\mathfrak{S}
-paths
\gamma
in the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
such that

\mathfrak{S}
chooses an action in
\mathcal{A}=\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathit{last}(\gamma))\setminus\mathit{Act}^{\min}(\mathit{last}(\gamma))
with positive probability after
\gamma
where
\mathit{last}(\gamma)
denotes the last state of
\gamma
.
Let

q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}P_{\mathcal{N}}(\gamma)\cdot\mathfrak{S}(\gamma)(\alpha).

So,
q
is the overall probability that a state-action pair from
\Pi
is chosen under
\mathfrak{S}
.
We now define a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
: For each action
\gamma\in\Gamma
ending in a state
s
and each action
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{N}}(s)\setminus\mathit{Act}^{\min}(s)
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
chooses action
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
in
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
with probability
P_{\mathcal{N}}(\gamma)\cdot\mathfrak{S}(\gamma)(\alpha)/q
.
When reaching a state
t
afterwards,
\mathfrak{T}
behaves like
\mathit{res}(\mathfrak{S},\gamma\,\alpha\,t)
afterwards.
Note that by definition this indeed defines a probability distribution over the actions in the initial state
\mathsf{init}_{\mathcal{K}}
.

By assumption, we know that now

2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}>0.

As the probability with which an action
\beta_{(s,\alpha)}
is chosen by
\mathfrak{T}
for a
(s,\alpha)\in\Pi
is
1/q
times the probability that
\alpha
is chosen in
s
to leave the sub-MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\min}_{E}
under
\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathcal{N}
and as the residual behavior is identical, we conclude that

\displaystyle 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{N}}=\,\,q\cdot(2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}})>0.

By Lemma
40
, this shows that
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
in
\mathcal{N}
and finishes the proof of Claim 2.

Construction of a game structure.

Recall the set of singleton SPR causes
\mathcal{C}
.
We now construct a
stochastic shortest path game
(see
[
PB99
]
) to check whether there is a subset
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
such that (
15
) holds in the EC-free MDP
\mathcal{K}
in which visiting effect states always has a non-zero probability.
Such a game is played on an MDP-like structure with the only difference that the set of states is partitioned into two sets indicating which player controls which states.

The game
\mathcal{G}
has states
(S_{\mathcal{K}}\times\{\mathit{yes},\mathit{no}\})\cup\mathcal{C}\times\{\mathit{choice}\}
.
On the subset
S_{\mathcal{K}}\times\{\mathit{yes}\}
, all actions and transition probabilities are just as in
\mathcal{K}
and this copy of
\mathcal{K}
cannot be left.
Formally, for all
s,t\in S_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)
, we have
\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{G}}((s,\mathit{yes}))=\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)
and
P_{\mathcal{G}}((s,\mathit{yes}),\alpha,(t,\mathit{yes}))=P_{\mathcal{K}}(s,\alpha,t)
.

In the “
\mathit{no}
”-copy, the game also behaves like
\mathcal{K}
but when a state in
\mathcal{C}
would be entered, the game moves to a state in
\mathcal{C}\times\{\mathit{choice}\}
instead.
In a state of the form
(c,\mathit{choice})
with
c\in\mathcal{C}
, two actions
\alpha
and
\beta
are available. Choosing
\alpha
leads to the state
(c,\mathit{yes})
while choosing
\beta
leads to
(c,\mathit{no})
with probability
1
.

Formally, this means that for all state
s\in S_{\mathcal{K}}
, we define
\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{G}}((s,\mathit{no}))=\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)
and for all actions
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)
:

•

P_{\mathcal{G}}((s,\mathit{no}),\alpha,(t,\mathit{no}))=P_{\mathcal{K}}(s,\alpha,t)

for all states
t\in S_{\mathcal{K}}\setminus\mathcal{C}

•

P_{\mathcal{G}}((s,\mathit{no}),\alpha,(c,\mathit{choice}))=P_{\mathcal{K}}(s,\alpha,c)

for all states
c\in\mathcal{C}

For states
s\in S_{\mathcal{K}}
,
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{K}}(s)
,
we furthermore define:

P_{\mathcal{G}}((c,\mathit{choice}),\alpha,(c,\mathit{yes}))=P_{\mathcal{G}}((c,\mathit{choice}),\beta,(c,\mathit{no}))=1
.

Intuitively speaking, whether a state
c\in\mathcal{C}
should belong to the cause set can be decided in the state
(c,\mathit{choice})
.
The “
\mathit{yes}
”-copy encodes that an effect state has been selected.
More concretely, the “
\mathit{yes}
-copy” is entered as soon as
\alpha
has been chosen in some state
(c,\mathit{choice})
and will never be left from then on.
The “
\mathit{no}
”-copy of
\mathcal{K}
then encodes that no state
c\in\mathcal{C}
which has been selected to become a cause state has been visited so far.
That is, if the current state of a play in
\mathcal{G}
belongs to the
\mathit{no}
-copy then in all previous decisions in the states
(c,\mathit{choice})
, action
\beta
has been chosen.

Finally, we equip the game with a weight structure.
All states in
\mathsf{Eff}\times\{\mathit{yes}\}
get weight
2(1-\vartheta)
. All remaining terminal states in
S_{\mathcal{K}}\times\{\mathit{yes}\}
get weight
-\vartheta
.
All states in
\mathsf{Eff}\times\{\mathit{no}\}
get weight
-\vartheta
.
All remaining states have weight
0
.

The game is played between two players
0
and
1
. Player
0
controls all states in
\mathcal{C}\times\{\mathit{choice}\}
while player
1
controls the remaining states.
The goal of player
0
is to ensure that the expected accumulated weight is
>0
.

Claim 3:

Player
0
has a winning strategy ensuring that the expected accumulated weight is
>0
in the game
\mathcal{G}
if and only if there is a subset
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
in
\mathcal{K}
which satisfies for all
c\in C:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{K}}(\neg C\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
and for all schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
we have

\displaystyle 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}>0.

(16)

Proof of Claim 3.
As
\mathcal{K}
has no end components, also in the game
\mathcal{G}
a terminal state is reached almost surely under any pair of strategies. Hence, we can rely on the results of
[
PB99
]
that state that both players have an optimal memoryless deterministic strategy.

We start by proving direction “
\Rightarrow
” of Claim 3. Let
\zeta
be a memoryless deterministic winning strategy for player
0
. I.e.,
\zeta
assigns to each state in
\mathcal{C}\times\{\mathit{choice}\}
an action from
\{\alpha,\beta\}
.
We define

\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{c\in\mathcal{C}\mid\zeta((c,\mathit{choice}))=\alpha\}.

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
is not empty as otherwise a positive expected accumulated weight in the game is not possible.
(Here we use the fact that only the effect states in the
\mathit{yes}
-copy have positive weight and that the
\mathit{yes}
-copy can only be entered by taking
\alpha
in one of the states
(c,\mathit{choice})
.)
To ensure for all
c\in C:\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{K}}(\neg C\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
, we remove states that cannot be visited as the first state of this set:

C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}\mid\mathcal{K},c\models\exists(\neg\mathcal{C}_{\alpha})\,\mathrm{U}\,c\}.

Note that the strategies for player
0
in
\mathcal{G}
which correspond to the sets
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
and
C
lead to exactly the same plays.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{K}
. This scheduler can be used as a strategy for player
1
in
\mathcal{G}
.
Let us denote the expected accumulated weight when player
0
plays according to
\zeta
and player
1
plays according to
\mathfrak{T}
by
w(\zeta,\mathfrak{T})
.
As
\zeta
is winning for player 0 we have

w(\zeta,\mathfrak{T})>0.

By the construction of the game, it follows directly that

w(\zeta,\mathfrak{T})=2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}.

Putting things together yields:

\displaystyle 2(1{-}\vartheta)\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}-\vartheta\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{K}}>0

(17)

For the other direction, suppose there is a set
C\subseteq\mathcal{C}
that satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{K}}(\neg C\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
for all
c\in C
and (
16
) for all schedulers
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{K}
.
We define the MD-strategy
\zeta
from
C
by letting
\zeta((c,\mathit{choice}))=\alpha
if and only if
c\in C
. For any strategy
\mathfrak{T}
for player
1
, we can again view
\mathfrak{T}
also as a scheduler for
\mathcal{K}
. Equation (
17
) holds again and shows that the expected accumulated weight in
\mathcal{G}
is positive if player
0
plays according to
\zeta
against any strategy for player
1
. This finishes the proof of Claim 3.

Putting together Claims 1-3.

We conclude that there is an SPR cause
C
in the original MDP
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathit{fscore}(C)>\vartheta
if and only if player
1
has a winning strategy in the constructed game
\mathcal{G}
.
As both players have optimal MD-strategies in
\mathcal{G}

[
PB99
]
, the decision problem is in
\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}
: We can guess the MD-strategy for player
0
and solve the resulting stochastic shortest path problem in polynomial time
[
BT91
]
to obtain an
\mathrm{NP}
-upper bound. Likewise, we can guess the MD-strategy for player
1
and solve the resulting stochastic shortest path problem to obtain the
\mathrm{coNP}
-upper bound.

Optimality and threshold constraints for GPR causes.

Computing optimal GPR causes for either quality measure can be done in polynomial space by considering all cause candidates, checking (G) in
\mathrm{coNP}
and computing the corresponding quality measure in polynomial time (Section
5.2
).
As the space can be reused after each computation, this results in polynomial space.
However, we show that no polynomial-time algorithms can be expected as the corresponding threshold problems are
\mathrm{NP}
-hard.
Let GPR-covratio (resp. GPR-recall, GPR-f-score) denote the decision
problems:
Given
\mathcal{M},\mathsf{Eff}
and
\vartheta\in\mathbb{Q}
, decide
whether there exists a GPR cause with
coverage ratio (resp. recall, f-score) at least
\vartheta
.

Theorem 41
.

The problems GPR-covratio, GPR-recall and GPR-f-score
are
\mathrm{NP}
-hard and belong to
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
.
For Markov chains, all three problems are
\mathrm{NP}
-complete.

\mathrm{NP}
-hardness even holds
for tree-like Markov chains.

Proof 5.13
.

\Sigma^{P}_{2}
-membership.
The algorithms for GPR-covratio, GPR-recall and GPR-f-score rely on the guess-and-check principle:
they start by non-deterministically guessing a set
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
,
then check in
\mathrm{coNP}
whether

\mathsf{Cause}
constitutes a GPR cause
(see Section
4
) and finally check
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})\leq\vartheta
(with standard techniques),
resp.
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})\leq\vartheta
,
resp.
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\leq\vartheta

(Theorem
31
)
in polynomial time.
The alternation between the existential quantification for guessing
\mathsf{Cause}
and the universal quantification for the
\mathrm{coNP}
check of the GPR condition results in the complexity
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
of the polynomial-time hierarchy.

\mathrm{NP}
-membership for Markov chains.

\mathrm{NP}
-membership for all three problems within Markov chains is straightforward as we
may non-deterministically guess a cause and check in
polynomial time whether it constitutes a GPR cause and satisfies the threshold
condition for the recall, coverage ratio or f-score.

\mathrm{NP}
-hardness of GPR-recall and GPR-covratio.
With arguments as in the proof of Lemma
35
,
the problems GPR-recall and GPR-covratio are polynomially interreducible
for Markov chains.
Thus, it suffices to prove NP-hardness of GPR-recall.
For this, we provide a polynomial reduction from the knapsack problem.
The input of the latter are sequences

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},A
and
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n},B
of positive natural numbers
and the task is to decide whether there exists a subset
I
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}A_{i}\ <\ A\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i\in I}B_{i}\ \geqslant\ B

(18)

Let
K
be the maximum of
A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},B,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
N=8(n{+}1)\cdot(K{+}1)
.
We then define

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{i}}{N},

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{N},

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{B_{i}}{N},

\displaystyle b=

\displaystyle\frac{B}{N}.

Then,
a,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b,b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}

are positive rational numbers strictly smaller than
\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}
,
and (
18
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\ <\ a\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}\ \geqslant\ b

(19)

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, let

p_{i}=2(a_{i}+b_{i})

and

w_{i}=\frac{b_{i}}{p_{i}}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{b_{i}}{a_{i}+b_{i}}
.
Then,

0<p_{i}<\frac{1}{2(n{+}1)}
and
0<w_{i}<\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover,
p_{i}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-w_{i}\bigr)=a_{i}
and
p_{i}\cdot w_{i}=b_{i}
.
Hence, (
19
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-w_{i}\bigr)\ <\ a\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}\ \geqslant\ b

which again is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}}{\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}}\ >\ \frac{1}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}\ \geqslant\ \ p_{0}+b

(20)

where
p_{0}=2a
,
w_{0}=1
and
I_{0}=I\cup\{0\}
.
Note that
a<\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}
and hence
p_{0}<\frac{1}{4(n{+}1)}
.

Define a tree-shape Markov chain
\mathcal{M}
with non-terminal states

\mathsf{init}
,
s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
,
and terminal states

\mathsf{eff}_{0},\ldots,\mathsf{eff}_{n}
,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
and

\mathsf{noeff},\mathsf{noeff}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{noeff}_{n}
.
Transition probabilities are as follows:

•

P(\mathsf{init},s_{i})=p_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,n

•

P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})\ =\ \frac{1}{2}-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}w_{i}

•

P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{noeff})=1-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}-P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})
,

•

P(s_{i},\mathsf{eff}_{i})=w_{i}
,
P(s_{i},\mathsf{noeff}_{i})=1{-}w_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n

•

P(s_{0},\mathsf{eff}_{0})=1=w_{0}
.

Note that
p_{0}+p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}<\frac{1}{2}
as all
p_{i}
’s are
strictly smaller
than
\frac{1}{2(n{+}1)}
. As the
w_{i}
’s are bounded by 1,
this yields
0<P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})<\frac{1}{2}
and

0<P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{noeff})<1
.

The graph structure of
\mathcal{M}
is indeed a tree and
\mathcal{M}
can be constructed from the values

A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
and
B,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
in polynomial time.
For
\mathsf{Eff}=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}\cup\{\mathsf{eff}_{i}:i=0,1,\ldots,n\}

we have:

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ \ =\ \ \sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}w_{i}+P(\mathsf{init},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}})\ \ =\ \ \frac{1}{2}.

As the values
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
are strictly smaller than
\frac{1}{2}
,
we have

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\ \Diamond C\ )<\frac{1}{2}

for each nonempty subset
C
of
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
.
Thus, the only candidates for GPR causes are the sets

C_{I}=\{s_{i}:i\in I_{0}\}
where
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}

where as before
I_{0}=I\cup\{0\}
.
Note that for all states
s\in C_{I}
there is a path satisfying

(\neg C_{I})\,\mathrm{U}\,s
. Thus,
C_{I}
is a GPR cause iff
C_{I}
satisfies (G).
We have:

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ |\ \Diamond C_{I}\ )\ \ =\ \ \frac{\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}}{\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}}

and

\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(C_{I})\ \ =\ \ \mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\ \Diamond(C_{I}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ |\ \Diamond\mathsf{Eff}\ )\ \ =\ \ 2\cdot\sum_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}.

Thus,
C_{I}
is a GPR cause with recall at least
2(p_{0}+b)

if and only if the two conditions in (
20
) hold,
which again is equivalent to the satisfaction of the conditions
in (
18
).
But this yields that
\mathcal{M}
has a GPR cause with recall at least

2(p_{0}+b)
if and only if the knapsack problem is solvable
for the input
A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},B,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
.

\mathrm{NP}
-hardness of GPR-f-score.
Using similar ideas, we also provide a polynomial reduction
from the knapsack problem.
Let
A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},B,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
be an input for the knapsack
problem.
We replace the
A
-sequence with
a,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
where

a=\frac{A}{N}
and
a_{i}=\frac{A_{i}}{N}

where
N
is as before.
The topological structure of the Markov chain that we are going to construct
is the same as in the NP-hardness proof for GPR-recall.

Next, we will consider the polynomial-time computable values

p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\ ]0,1[
(where
p_{i}=P(\mathsf{init},s_{i})
),

w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in\ ]0,1[
(where
w_{i}=P(s_{i},\mathsf{eff}_{i})
)
and auxiliary variables
\delta\in\ ]0,1[
and
\lambda>1

such that:

(1)

p_{0}+p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}<\frac{1}{2}

(2)

\lambda=\frac{p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}-\delta}{p_{0}}

(3)

for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
:

(3.1)

a_{i}\ =\ p_{i}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-w_{i})

(in particular
w_{i}<\frac{1}{2}
)

(3.2)

B_{i}\ =\ \frac{1}{\delta}Bp_{i}\bigl(\lambda w_{i}-1)

(in particular
w_{i}>\frac{1}{\lambda}
)

Assuming such values have been defined, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}B_{i}\ \geqslant\ B

iff

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}B\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}(\lambda w_{i}-1)\ \geqslant\ B

iff

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}(\lambda w_{i}-1)\ \geqslant\ \delta

iff

\displaystyle\lambda\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}\ \geqslant\ \delta+\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}

Hence:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}B_{i}\ \geqslant\ B

iff

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}}{\displaystyle\delta+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}}\ \geqslant\ \frac{1}{\lambda}

For all positive real numbers
x,y,u,v
with
\frac{x}{y}=\frac{1}{\lambda}

we have:

\frac{x+u}{y+v}\geqslant\frac{1}{\lambda}\ \ \ \ \ \text{iff}\ \ \ \ \ \frac{u}{v}\geqslant\frac{1}{\lambda}

By the constraints for
\lambda
(see (2)), we have

\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}-\delta}=\frac{1}{\lambda}
.
Therefore:

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}}{\displaystyle\delta+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}}\ \geqslant\ \frac{1}{\lambda}

iff

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle p_{0}+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}}{\displaystyle(p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}-\delta)+\delta+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}}\ \ =\ \ \frac{\displaystyle p_{0}+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}w_{i}}{\displaystyle p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}+\sum\limits_{i\in I}p_{i}}\ \geqslant\ \frac{1}{\lambda}

As before let
w_{0}=1
and
I_{0}=I\cup\{0\}
. Then,
the above yields:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}B_{i}\ \geqslant\ B

iff

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}}{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}}\ \geqslant\ \frac{1}{\lambda}

As in the NP-hardness proof for GPR-recall and using (3.1):

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\ >\ w_{i}\qquad\text{for $i=1,\ldots,n$}

Thus,
each GPR cause must have the form
C_{I}=\{s_{i}:i\in I_{0}\}

for some subset
I
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Moreover:

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C_{I})\ =\ \sum_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(C_{I}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))\ =\ \sum_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}

So, the f-score of
C_{I}
is:

\mathit{fscore}(C_{I})\ \ =\ \ 2\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond(C_{I}\wedge\Diamond\mathsf{Eff}))}{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})+\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond C_{I})}\ \ =\ \ 2\cdot\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}w_{i}}{\frac{1}{2}+\sum\limits_{i\in I_{0}}p_{i}}

This implies:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}B_{i}\ \geqslant\ B

iff

\displaystyle\mathit{fscore}(C_{I})\ \geqslant\ \frac{2}{\lambda}

With
p_{0}=2a
and using (3.1) and arguments as in the NP-hardness proof
for GPR-recall, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}A_{i}\ <\ A

iff

C_{I}
is a GPR cause

Thus, the constructed Markov chain has a GPR cause with f-score at least

\frac{2}{\lambda}
if and only if the knapsack problem is solvable
for the input
A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},B,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
.

It remains to define the values
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
and

\delta
. (The value of
\lambda
is then obtained by (2).)
(3.1) and (3.2) can be rephrased as equations for
w_{i}
:

(3.1’)
:

w_{i}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}

(3.2’)
:

w_{i}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\bigl(\delta\frac{B_{i}}{Bp_{i}}+1\bigr)

This yields an equation for
p_{i}
:

\frac{1}{2}-\frac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}\ \ =\ \ \frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigl(\delta\frac{B_{i}}{Bp_{i}}+1\Bigr)

and leads to:

\displaystyle p_{i}\ \ =\ \ \frac{2\lambda}{\lambda-2}a_{i}\ +\ \frac{2\delta}{\lambda-2}\cdot\frac{B_{i}}{B}

(21)

We now substitute
\lambda
by (2) and arrive at

p_{i}\ \ =\ \ \frac{p_{0}}{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}a_{i}\ +\ a_{i}+\ \frac{\delta p_{0}}{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\frac{B_{i}}{B}.

By choice of
N
, all
a_{i}
’s and
a
are smaller than
\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}
.
Using this together with
p_{0}=2a
, we get:

\displaystyle p_{i}\ <\ \frac{1}{4(n{+}1)(\frac{1}{2}-\delta)}\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}\ +\ \frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}\ +\ \frac{\delta}{4(n{+}1)(\frac{1}{2}-\delta)}\frac{B_{i}}{B}

(22)

Let now
\delta=\frac{1}{8K}
(where
K
is as above, i.e.,
the maximum of the values
A,A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
,
B
,
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
).
Then,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are computable
in polynomial time, and so are the values
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}

(by (3.1’)).
As
\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda-2}>2
and using (
21
),
we obtain

p_{i}>2a_{i}
.
So, by (3.1’) we get
0<w_{i}<\frac{1}{2}
.

It remains to prove (1).
Using
\delta=\frac{1}{8K}
, we obtain from (
22
):

p_{i}\ <\ \frac{1}{4(n{+}1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8K})}\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}+\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}+\frac{1}{32(n{+}1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8K})K}\frac{B_{i}}{B}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ x

As
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8K}\geq\frac{1}{4}
and
\frac{B_{i}}{B}<K
, this yields:

p_{i}\ <\ x\ <\ \frac{1}{8(n{+}1)^{2}}+\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}+\frac{1}{8(n{+}1)}\ <\ \frac{1}{2(n{+}1)}.

But then condition (1) holds.

Arbitrary quality measures

Consider any algebraic function
f(\mathsf{tp},\mathsf{tn},\mathsf{fp},\mathsf{fn})
.
That is
f
satisfies some polynomial equation where the coefficients are polynomials in
\mathsf{tp},\mathsf{tn},\mathsf{fp}
and
\mathsf{fn}
.
Almost every quality measure for binary classifiers (see
[
Pow11
]
) is such a function.
Taking the worst case scheduler for such a function we define

f(\mathsf{Cause})=\inf_{\mathfrak{S}}f^{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathsf{tp}_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\mathfrak{S}},\mathsf{tn}_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\mathfrak{S}},\mathsf{fp}_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\mathfrak{S}},\mathsf{fn}_{\mathsf{Cause}}^{\mathfrak{S}}),

where
\mathfrak{S}
ranges over all schedulers such that
f^{\mathfrak{S}}
is well defined.
Given a PR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
and a rational
\vartheta\in\mathbb{Q}
, deciding whether
f(\mathsf{Cause})\leq\vartheta
can be done in
\mathrm{PSPACE}
as a satisfiability problem in the existential theory of the reals
[
Can88
]
.

As we can decide for a given cause candidate
\mathsf{Cause}
whether it is a SPR cause in
\mathrm{P}
or a GPR cause in
\mathrm{coNP}
, this also yields an algorithm for finding optimal causes for
f
.
Given an MDP
\mathcal{M}
with terminal effect set
\mathsf{Eff}
and quality measure
f
as an algebraic function we consider each cause candidate
\mathsf{Cause}
, check whether it is a PR cause (SPR or GPR) and consider the decision problem
f(\mathsf{Cause})\leq\vartheta
.
As all of these steps have a complexity upper bound of
\mathrm{PSPACE}
and we only need to save the best cause candidate so far with its value
f(\mathsf{Cause})
, this results in polynomial space as well.

6.
\omega
-regular effect scenarios

In this section, we turn to an extension of the previous definition of PR causes.
So far, we considered both the cause and the effect as sets of states in an MDP
\mathcal{M}
with state space
S
. We will refer to this setting as the
state-based
setting from now on.
In a more general approach, we now consider the effect to be an
\omega
-regular language
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
over the state space
S
.
Note that we denote regular events as effects mainly by
\mathsf{rEff}
to avoid confusion with effects as sets of states.

In a first step, we still consider sets of states
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
as causes, which we call
reachability causes
(Section
6.1
).
For reachability GPR causes, the techniques from the previous section are mostly still applicable. For reachability SPR causes, on the other hand, we observe that they take on the flavor of state-based GPR causes as well.
Afterwards, we generalize the definition further to allow

\omega
-regular co-safety properties over the state space
S
as causes, which we call
co-safety causes
(Section
6.2
). While this allows us to express much more involved cause-effect relationships, we will see that attempts of checking co-safety SPR causality or of finding good causes for a given effect encounter major new difficulties.

6.1.
Sets of states as causes

Throughout this section, let
\mathcal{M}=(S,\mathit{Act},P,\mathsf{init})
be an MDP.
As long as we use sets of states as causes, the definition of GPR and SPR causes can easily be adapted to
\omega
-regular effects:

{defi}
[Reachability GPR/SPR causes]

Let
\mathcal{M}
be as above. Let
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
be an
\omega
-regular language over
S
and
\mathsf{Cause}
a nonempty subset of
S
such that for each
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
.
Then,
\mathsf{Cause}
is said to be a
reachability GPR cause
for

\mathsf{rEff}
iff the following condition (rG) holds:

(rG):

For each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
where
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Diamond\mathsf{Cause})>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\ |\ \Diamond\mathsf{Cause})\ >\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}).

(
rGPR
)

\mathsf{Cause}
is called a
reachability SPR cause
for
\mathsf{rEff}
iff the following condition (rS) holds:

(rS):

For each state
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
and each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
where
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}((\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid(\neg\mathsf{Cause})\,\mathrm{U}\,c)\ >\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}).

(
rSPR
)

There is one small caveat that we want to mention here: If the effect
\mathsf{rEff}
is a reachability property
\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}
for a set of states
\mathsf{Eff}\subseteq S
,
then this new definition allows for GPR/SPR causes
\mathsf{Cause}
not disjoint from the set of states
\mathsf{Eff}
.
If two sets
\mathsf{Cause},\mathsf{Eff}\subseteq S
are disjoint, however, then
\mathsf{Cause}
is a GPR/SPR cause for
\mathsf{Eff}
according to Definition
3
iff

\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability GPR/SPR cause for the
\omega
-regular event
\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}
according to the new definition.
As we now view the effect as the
\omega
-regular property on infinite executions, one can, nevertheless, argue that
the
temporal priority

(C2)
is captured by the new definition since the cause will be reached after finitely many steps if it is reached.
We will address problems with this interpretation and a stronger notion of temporal priority in Section
6.1.3
.

A first simple observation that follows as in the state-based setting is that a reachability SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
is also a reachability GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}

6.1.1.
Checking causality and existence of reachability PR causes

To explore how this change of definition influences the previously established results for GPR and SPR causes, we have to clarify how effects will be represented.
We use deterministic Rabin automata (DRAs) as they are expressive enough to capture all
\omega
-regular languages and they are deterministic, which will allow us to form well-behaved products of the automata with MDPs.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP,
\mathsf{rEff}
an effect given by the DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
and
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
a cause candidate.
As a special case we again have Markov chains with no non-deterministic choices.
Then, the conditions (rG) and (rS) can easily be checked by computing the corresponding probabilities in polynomial time (see
[
BKKM14
]
for algorithms to compute conditional probabilities in MCs for path properties).
We now consider the case where non-deterministic choices exist.
We will provide a model transformation of
\mathcal{M}
using the DRA such that the resulting MDP has no end components and the effect is a reachability property again.

{nota}

[Removing end components]

Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
be as above.
Consider the product MDP
\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
This product is an MDP equipped with a Rabin acceptance condition found in the second component of each state in the product.

We now take two copies of
\mathcal{N}
representing a mode before
\mathsf{Cause}
has been reached and one mode after
\mathsf{Cause}
has been reached.
So, each state
\mathfrak{s}
is equipped with one extra bit
0
or
1
.
Initially, the MDP starts in the copy labeled with
0
and behaves like
\mathcal{N}
until a state with its first component in
\mathsf{Cause}
is reached.
From there, the process moves to the corresponding successor states in the second copy labeled with
1
.
We call the resulting MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and denote the set of states with their first component in
\mathsf{Cause}
in the first copy that are reachable in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
by
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
, in particular, to express that these states are enriched with states of the automaton
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.

Next, we consider the MECs
\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
Note that the states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
are not contained in any MEC.
Furthermore, all MECs consist either only of states from the first copy labeled
0
, or only of states from the second copy labeled with
1
.
For each MEC
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we determine whether there is a scheduler for
\mathcal{E}_{i}
that ensures the event
\mathit{Acc}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}})
that the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
is met with probability
1
and whether there is a scheduler that ensures this event with probability
0
.
With the techniques of
[
dA97
]
and
[
BGC09
]
this can be done in polynomial time.
We then add four new terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
,
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
, and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
and construct the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
while, for each
i\leq k
, enabling a new action in the state
s_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
obtained from
\mathcal{E}_{i}
leading to

•

\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
if
\mathit{Acc}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}})
can be ensured with probability
1
in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is contained in copy
0
, and

•

another new action leading to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
if
\mathit{Acc}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}})
can be ensured with probability
0
in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is contained in copy
0
;

•

\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
if
\mathit{Acc}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}})
can be ensured with probability
1
in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is contained in copy
1
, and

•

another new action leading to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
if
\mathit{Acc}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}})
can be ensured with probability
0
in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is contained in copy
1
.

Finally, we remove all states which are not reachable (from the initial state).
We call the resulting MDP
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
and emphasize that this MDP contains all states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
, has no end components, and has the four terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
,
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
, and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
. Furthermore, for each
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, we denote the subset of states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
with
c
in their first component by
c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
.

\lhd

Lemma 42
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
be as above and let
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
be the constructed MDP in Notation
6.1.1
which contains the set
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
.
Then,
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is a GPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
.
Furthermore,
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if, for each
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, the set
c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
of states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
with
c
in their first component is a GPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
.

Proof 6.1
.

The set
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,d)>0
for each state
d\in\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
by construction, since all states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
are reachable and a run cannot reach two different states in
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
.
So, this minimality condition is satisfied in any case and, of course,
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
and
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
are disjoint.

Now, let
\mathfrak{T}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
.
The scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
can be mimicked by a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
:
As long as
\mathfrak{T}
moves through the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
follows this behavior by leaving MECs through the corresponding actions in
\mathcal{M}
.
Whenever
\mathfrak{T}
moves to one of the states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
, the last step begins in a state
s_{\mathcal{E}}
obtained from a MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
.
In this case,
\mathfrak{S}
will stay in
\mathcal{E}
while ensuring with probability
1
that the resulting run is accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Similarly, if
\mathfrak{T}
moves to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
or
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
stays in the corresponding MEC and realizes the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
with probability
0
.
Vice versa, a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
can be mimicked by a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
analogously.
So in an end component
\mathcal{E}
, if
\mathfrak{S}
stays in
\mathcal{E}
and ensures that the resulting run is accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
with probability
p_{1}
,
stays in
\mathcal{E}
and ensures that the resulting run is not accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
with probability
p_{2}
,
and leaves
\mathcal{E}
with probability
p_{3}
,

\mathfrak{T}
will move to the corresponding state
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
or
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
with probability
p_{1}
, to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
or
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
with probability
p_{2}
, and take actions leaving
s_{\mathcal{E}}
to other states with the same probability distribution with which
\mathfrak{S}
takes the leaving actions of
\mathcal{E}
.

For such a pair of schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{T}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}),

(23)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge E),\text{ and}

(24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\mathsf{rEff})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\wedge\lozenge E),

(25)

where
E=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
.
Hence,
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is a GPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
if and only if
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

Now consider each element
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
individually.
We can use the same argumentation to see that a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
can be mimicked by a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
, and vice versa,
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}\mid\lozenge c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}).

Thus,
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if
c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is a GPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
for all
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
.

This Lemma
42
shows that reachability SPR causality shares similarities with GPR causality.
Our algorithmic results for reachability PR causes stem from the reduction provided by Notation
6.1.1
and Lemma
42
.
As an immediate consequence we can check conditions (rG) and (rS) in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
by using the already established algorithms for GPR causes.
This results in the following complexity upper bounds.

Corollary 43
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
an
\omega
-regular language given as DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Given a set
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
we can decide
whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability SPR/GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathrm{coNP}
.

Proof 6.2
.

By Lemma
42
, we can use the construction of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
, which takes polynomial time.
Then, Theorem
17
allows us to check whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability GPR cause in
\mathrm{coNP}
directly, while we can apply the GPR check to each set
c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
and the effect
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
for
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
in order to determine whether
\mathsf{Cause}
is a reachability SPR
\mathsf{Cause}
for
\mathsf{rEff}
.

As in the state-based setting, we can argue that there is a reachability GPR cause iff there is a reachability SPR cause iff there is a singleton reachability SPR cause.
Consequently, the existence of a reachability GPR/SPR cause can be checked by checking for each state
c
of the MDP whether
\{c\}
constitutes a reachability SPR cause.
We conclude:

Corollary 44
.

The existence of a reachability GPR/SPR cause can be decided in
\mathrm{coNP}
.

6.1.2.
Computing quality measures of reachability PR causes

As in Section
5.1
, we can view reachability PR causes as binary classifiers.
This leads to the confusion matrix as before (Figure
13
) with the difference that this time the path does not “hit” the effect set, but rather
\mathsf{rEff}
holds on the path.
Hence, we define the following entries of the confusion matrix:
Given
\mathsf{rEff}
,
\mathsf{Cause}
and a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
, we let

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\mathsf{rEff}),&\mathsf{tn}^{\mathfrak{S}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\mathsf{rEff}),\\[4.30554pt]
\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\neg\mathsf{rEff}),&\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\neg\lozenge\mathsf{Cause}\wedge\mathsf{rEff}).\end{array}

Lemma 45
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be as above,
\mathsf{rEff}
an
\omega
-regular effect given by the DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
and let
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
be a reachability GPR/SPR cause. Further, let

\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
be as constructed above with the set of cause states
\mathsf{Cause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
and the set of effect states
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
.
Then, for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
, there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
, and vice versa, such that

\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{T}},\qquad\mathsf{tn}^{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathsf{tn}^{\mathfrak{T}},\qquad\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{T}},\qquad\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{T}},

where the values for
\mathfrak{T}
are defined in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
as for the state-based setting (cf. Section
5.1
).

Proof 6.3
.

In the proof of Lemma
42
, we have seen that for each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
, we can find a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
and vice versa
such that Equations (
23
) - (
25
) hold. This implies the equalities claimed here.

Analogously to Section
5.1
, we can now define
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}
,
\mathit{covrat}
, and
\mathit{fscore}
of a reachability PR cause as the infimum of these values in terms of

\mathsf{tp}^{\mathfrak{S}}
,
\mathsf{fp}^{\mathfrak{S}}
,
\mathsf{tn}^{\mathfrak{S}}
, and
\mathsf{fn}^{\mathfrak{S}}
over all schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
for which the respective quality measures are defined.
The computation of these values can then be done with the methods from the state-based setting:

Corollary 46
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
an
\omega
-regular language given as DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Given a reachability SPR/GPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
we can compute
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause}),\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})
and
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
in polynomial time.

Proof 6.4
.

Use Lemma
45
, Corollary
30
and Theorem
31
.

6.1.3.
Finding quality optimal reachability PR causes

When trying to find good causes for an
\omega
-regular effect
\mathsf{rEff}
, we cannot say that effects and causes should be disjoint as in the setting where effects and causes were sets of states.
This leads to the possibility that causes might exist that do not capture the intuition behind temporal priority: E.g., if the effect
\mathsf{rEff}
is a reachability property
\lozenge E
for a set of states
E
, the set of states
E
itself will be a reachability PR cause if for each state
c\in E
,
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg E\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
holds. Furthermore, there might be causes
C
that can only be reached after
E
has already been reached.

In order to account for the temporal priority of causes, we will include the following condition when trying to find good causes:
We require for a cause
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)<1\text{ for all $c\in\mathsf{Cause}$.}

(TempPrio)

Intuitively, this states that it is never already certain that the execution will belong to
\mathsf{rEff}
when the cause is reached.
A variation of this criterium has been proposed also in
[
BDF+21
]
.

Remark 47
.

The condition (
TempPrio
) could be added to the definition of reachability PR causes. After the product construction in Notation
6.1.1
, the condition can easily be checked for a given cause candidate
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
: For each
c\in\mathsf{Cause}
, there must be at least one state with
c
in the first component in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
such that
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
is reachable from this state.

Furthermore, this condition is stronger than the requirement that causes and effects are disjoint in the state-based setting. In the state-based setting, however, the analogue of condition (
TempPrio
) could also be included easily. Instead of having to be disjoint from a set of effect states
\mathsf{Eff}
, a cause
\mathsf{Cause}
would then simply not be allowed to contain any state
s
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M},s}(\Diamond\mathsf{Eff})=1
.
\lhd

Now, we want to find recall- and coverage ratio-optimal reachability SPR causes.
As in the state-based setting, we define the set
\mathcal{C}
of all possible singleton reachability SPR causes for
\mathsf{rEff}

that in addition satisfy (
TempPrio
) as explained in Remark
47
.
By Corollary
43
, we can check whether a state
c\in S
is a singleton reachability SPR cause in coNP; whether there is at least one state with
c
in the first component in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
such that
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
is reachable from this state is checkable in polynomial time.
Thus, we can again define the set of singleton reachability SPR causes

\mathcal{C}=\{s\in S\mid s\text{ is reachability SPR cause satisfying }\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)<1\}.

As before, the canonical cause
\mathsf{CanCause}
is now the set of states
c\in\mathcal{C}
for which there is a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}(\neg\mathcal{C}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)
.

For the complexity of the computation of the recall- and coverage ratio-optimal canonical cause and its values, the observations above lead us to the complexity class

\mathrm{PF}^{\mathrm{NP}}
as defined in
[
Sel94
]
. It consists of all functions that can be computed in polynomial time with access to an
\mathrm{NP}
-oracle, or equivalently a
\mathrm{coNP}
-oracle.

Proposition 48
.

If
\mathcal{C}\neq\emptyset
then
\mathsf{CanCause}
is a ratio- and recall optimal reachability SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
.
The threshold problem for the coverage ratio and the recall can be decided in
\mathrm{coNP}
.
The optimal values
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{CanCause})
and
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{CanCause})
can be computed in
\mathrm{PF}^{\mathrm{NP}}
.

Proof 6.5
.

The optimality of
\mathsf{CanCause}
follows by the arguments used for Theorem
36
.
In order to compute
\mathsf{CanCause}
we check for each state
s
whether (rS) does not hold in
\mathrm{NP}
and then take the remaining states
c\in S
to define
\mathcal{C}
by checking (
TempPrio
) for each
c
.
If (
TempPrio
) holds then
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathsf{CanCause}=\{c\in\mathcal{C}\mid\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathcal{C}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0\}
.
This allows to compute the recall- and coverage ratio-optimal cause
\mathsf{CanCause}
in
\mathrm{PF}^{\mathrm{NP}}
.

For the threshold problem whether there is a reachability SPR cause with recall at least a given
\vartheta\in\mathbb{Q}
, the
\mathrm{coNP}
upper bound can be shown as follows:
For each state
c
that does not belong to
\mathcal{C}
, i.e., that is not a singleton reachability SPR cause, there is a polynomial size certificate for this, as it can be checked in
\mathrm{coNP}
.
The collection of all states that do not belong to
\mathcal{C}
together with these certificates (they do not belong to
\mathcal{C}
) can now serve as a certificate that the optimal recall is less than
\vartheta
in this the case.
Given such a collection of certificates, we can check in polynomial time that indeed all provided states do not belong to
\mathcal{C}
.
The complement of the provided states forms a super set
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathsf{CanCause}
.
Computing the recall of the set
\mathcal{D}
can then be done in polynomial time as in Corollary
46
. This value is an upper bound for the recall of
\mathsf{CanCause}
. Note that if all states that do not belong to
\mathcal{C}
are given in the certificate, the value even equals the recall of
\mathsf{CanCause}
.

So, if the optimal value is less than
\vartheta
, this is witnessed by the described certificate containing all states not in
\mathcal{C}
.
Vice versa, if a certificate is given resulting in a super set
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathsf{CanCause}
such that the recall of
\mathcal{D}
is less than
\vartheta
, then there is no reachability SPR cause with a recall of at least
\vartheta
. So, the threshold problem lies in
\mathrm{coNP}
. For the coverage ratio, the analogous argument works.

For the threshold problems for f-score-optimal reachability SPR causes and reachability GPR causes optimal with respect to recall, coverage ration or f-score that satisfy (
TempPrio
),
we rely on the guess-and-check approach used for optimal GPR causes in the state-based setting.
We guess a subset
\mathsf{Cause}
of states of the MDP, check whether we found a reachability SPR/GPR cause in
\mathrm{coNP}
