, and compute the quality measure under consideration in polynomial time as explained in the previous section.
For the computation of an optimal cause, we obtain a polynomial-space algorithm.

Corollary 49
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\mathsf{rEff}
be an
\omega
-regular language given by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Given
\vartheta\in\mathbb{Q}
, deciding whether there exists a reachability GPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
with
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta
(resp.
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta,\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta
) can be done in
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
and is
\mathrm{NP}
-hard.

\mathrm{NP}
-hardness even holds for Markov chains.

Deciding whether there is a reachability SPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
with
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta
can be done in
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
.
A recall-, covratio-, or f-score-optimal reachability GPR cause as well as an f-score-optimal reachability SPR cause can be computed in polynomial space.

Proof 6.6
.

Obviously the lower bounds extend to this setting as we can interpret GPR causes for
\mathsf{Eff}
as reachability GPR causes for
\lozenge\mathsf{Eff}
.
The upper bounds extend to this setting by using the construction from Notation
6.1.1
.
Since for Theorem
41
we relied on guess-and-check algorithms to establish the upper bounds for the threshold problems, we can use analogous algorithms in setting of
\omega
-regular effects.
We guess a set
\mathsf{Cause}\subseteq S
, check the reachability GPR causality in
\mathrm{coNP}
(Corollary
43
) and compute the value of the quality measure in polynomial time (Corollary
46
).
Again, the alternation between the existential quantification for guessing
\mathsf{Cause}
and the universal quantification for the
\mathrm{coNP}
check results in the complexity
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
of the polynomial-time hierarchy.

In order to show that the decision problem for
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta
for SPR causes
\mathsf{Cause}
is in
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
we resort to the constructed MDP
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
.
By Lemma
42
a reachability SPR cause
\mathsf{Cause}
in
\mathcal{M}
corresponds to a set of GPR causes
\{c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\mid c\in\mathsf{Cause}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
, which can be interpreted as GPR cause
C=\bigcup_{c\in\mathsf{Cause}}c_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
.
This way we can encode the property
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})\geq\vartheta
in
\mathcal{M}
by
\mathit{fscore}(C)\geq\vartheta
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{Cause}]}
.
This results in a decision problem GPR-f-score for GPR causes which is in
\Sigma^{P}_{2}
by Theorem
41
.

For computing optimal GPR causes as well as f-score-optimal SPR causes we can try all cause candidates by computing the related value (
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}},\mathit{covrat}
or
\mathit{fscore}
) and always store the best one so far.
As the space for the cause can be reused, this results in a polynomial space algorithm.

6.2.
\omega
-regular co-safety properties as causes

We now want to discuss an extension of the previous framework when we also consider causes to be regular sets of executions.
However, in order to account for the
temporal priority
of causes, i.e., the fact that causes should occur before their effects, it makes sense to restrict causes to
\omega
-regular co-safety properties.
The reason is that an
\omega
-regular co-safety property
\mathcal{L}
is uniquely determined by the regular set of minimal
good
prefixes of words in
\mathcal{L}
.
Recall that a good prefix
\pi
for
\mathcal{L}
is a finite word such that all infinite extensions of
\pi
belong to
\mathcal{L}
and that all infinite words in the co-safety language
\mathcal{L}
have a good prefix.
Hence, we can say that a cause
\mathsf{rCause}
occurred as soon as a good prefix for
\mathsf{rCause}
has been produced.
For this subsection we will denote regular effects and causes mainly by
\mathsf{rEff}
and
\mathsf{rCause}
to avoid confusion with effects and causes as sets of states.
In the following formal definition, we use finite words
\sigma\in S^{\ast}
to denote the event
\sigma S^{\omega}
.

{defi}
[co-safety GPR/SPR causes]

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP with state space
S
and let
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
be an
\omega
-regular language.
An
\omega
-regular co-safety language
\mathsf{rCause}\subseteq S^{\omega}
is a
co-safety GPR cause
for
\mathsf{rEff}
if the following condition (coG) holds:

(coG):

For each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
where

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause})>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\mathsf{rCause})\ >\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}).

(
cosafeGPR
)

The event
\mathsf{rCause}
is called a
co-safety SPR cause
for

\mathsf{rEff}
if the following condition (coS) hold:

(coS):

For each minimal good prefix
\sigma
for
\mathsf{rCause}
and each scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
where
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\sigma)>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\sigma)\ >\ \mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}).

(
cosafeSPR
)

As in the state-based setting it follows that co-safety SPR cause are also co-safety GPR causes.

6.2.1.
Checking co-safety causality

We will represent co-safety PR causes as DFAs which accept good prefixes of the represented
\omega
-regular event.
Note that, for any
\omega
-regular co-safety property, there is a DFA accepting exactly the minimal good prefixes.
So, we will restrict to such DFAs that accept the minimal good prefixes of an
\omega
-regular co-safety property.
Such a DFA can never accept a word
w
as well as a proper prefix
v
of
w
.

Let now
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP,
\mathsf{rEff}
an effect given by the DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
and
\mathsf{rCause}
a cause candidate given by a DFA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
as above.
So, in particular,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
accepts exactly the minimal good prefixes for
\mathsf{rCause}
.
We now want to check, whether
\mathsf{rCause}
is a co-safety SPR cause (resp. co-safety GPR cause).
For the special case of Markov chains the check can be done in polynomial time analogously to reachability PR causes by computing the corresponding conditional probabilities.
We can provide a model transformation of
\mathcal{M}
using both automata such that the resulting MDP has no end components and the effect is a reachability property again similar to Notation
6.1.1
.

For this consider the product
\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
.
This product is an MDP equipped with two kinds of acceptance conditions.
The Rabin acceptance of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
in the second component of each state and the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
in the third component.
Now let
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
be the set of all states of
\mathcal{N}
whose third component is accepting in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
and which are reachable from the initial state.

As in Notation
6.1.1
, we construct an MDP
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
by introducing a mode before
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
and a mode after
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
. We then take the MEC-quotient with the four terminal states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
,
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
,
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
, and
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
, which are reachable from states
s_{\mathcal{E}}
that result from collapsing the MEC
\mathcal{E}
depending on whether
\mathcal{E}
is contained in the before- or after-
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
mode and whether the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
can be realized with probability
0
and
1
, respectively in
\mathcal{E}
, analogously to Notation
6.1.1
.
We call the resulting MDP
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
and emphasize that this MDP still contains all states in
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
as they are not contained in any end component.

We start with the observation, that for co-safety GPR causes this reduction characterizes the condition (coG) completely.

Lemma 50
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be and MDP,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
an DRA, and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
a DFA be as above and let
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
be the constructed MDP that contains the set
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
of reachable states that have an accepting
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
-component.
Then,
\mathsf{rCause}
is a co-safety GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
if and only if the set of states
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is a GPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
.

Proof 6.7
.

The set
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\neg\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
for each
c\in\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
by construction, since all states in
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
are reachable and a run cannot reach two different states in
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
.
Thus, the minimality condition is satisfied in any case.

Now, let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
.
The scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
can be mimicked by a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}
:
As long as
\mathfrak{S}
moves through the MEC-quotient of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
follows this behavior by leaving MECs through the corresponding actions in
\mathcal{M}
.
Whenever
\mathfrak{S}
moves to one of the states
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}}
and
\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}
, the last step begins in a state
s_{\mathcal{E}}
obtained from a MEC
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{M}
. In this case,
\mathfrak{T}
will stay in
\mathcal{E}
while ensuring with probability
1
that the resulting run is accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Similarly, if
\mathfrak{S}
moves to
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{tn}}
or
\mathsf{noeff}_{\mathsf{fp}}
, the scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}
stays in the corresponding MEC and realizes the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
with probability
0
.
Vice versa, a scheduler
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
can be mimicked by a scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
analogously (see also the proof of Lemma
42
).

For such a pair of schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{T}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}),

(26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge E),\text{ and}

(27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause}\land\mathsf{rEff})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\land\lozenge E),

(28)

where
E=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
.
Hence,
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is a GPR cause for
E
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
if and only if
\mathsf{rCause}
is a regular GPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

For co-safety SPR causes this is not a full characterization but only holds in one direction:

Lemma 51
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
be as above and let
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
be the constructed MDP that contains the set
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
of reachable states that have an accepting
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
-component.
If
\mathsf{rCause}
is a co-safety SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
, then the set of states
\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
is an SPR cause for
\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
.

Proof 6.8
.

Suppose
\mathsf{rCause}
is a regular SPR cause for
\mathsf{rEff}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
c\in\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}
and let
\mathfrak{T}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
such that
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\neg\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
.
Let

\Pi=\{\pi\text{ a path in $\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}$}\mid\pi\vDash\neg\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,c\text{ and $\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\pi)>0$}\}.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a scheduler for
\mathcal{M}
mimicking
\mathfrak{T}
as described in the proof of Lemma
50
.
Let
\Sigma_{\pi}
be the set of
\mathfrak{T}
-paths in
\mathcal{M}
that correspond to the path
\pi
in
\Pi
.
I.e., a path
\sigma
belongs to
\Sigma_{\pi}
if it moves through the MECs of
\mathcal{M}
in the same way as the path
\pi
moves through the MEC-quotient until
\sigma
and
\pi
reach the state
c
and if furthermore,
\sigma
has positive probability under
\mathfrak{S}
.
Now,

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge E\mid\neg\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)=\frac{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\pi}}\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\sigma)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\sigma)}{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\pi}}\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\sigma)},

where
E=\{\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{cov}},\mathsf{eff}_{\mathsf{unc}}\}
.
All the terms
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\sigma)
are greater than the value of the term
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff})=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge E)
.
Hence, we conclude that

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge E\mid\neg\mathsf{rCause}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{T}}_{\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}}(\lozenge E).

For checking co-safety SPR causality this is not sufficient.
The underlying problem, in which co-safety SPR causes and SPR causes differ, can be seen in the following example:

\mathsf{init}
b
d
c
t
\mathsf{noeff}
\mathsf{eff}
1/2
1/4
1/4
3/4
1/4
\beta
1/2
1/2
\alpha

Figure 17.
An MDP
\mathcal{M}
for which the reduction from co-safety to state-based PR causes fails

{exa}

Consider the MDP
\mathcal{M}
from Figure
17
with
\mathsf{rEff}=\lozenge\mathsf{eff}
.
For every scheduler
\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge c)>0
we have

\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}\mid\lozenge c)>\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff})

and thus
c
is a state-based SPR cause for
\mathsf{eff}
.
On the other hand for the scheduler
\tau
, which chooses
\alpha
after the path
\pi=\mathsf{init}\;b\;c
and
\beta
otherwise ,we have

\mathrm{Pr}^{\tau}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}\mid\pi)=\frac{1}{2}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\tau}_{\mathcal{M}}(\lozenge\mathsf{eff}).

Therefore, the desired reduction as in Lemma
50
does not work for
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that the violation of the condition (coS) is only possible in this example if the scheduler behaves differently depending on how state
c
is reached. This different behavior, however, does not have anything to do with the effect, and potentially different residual properties that have to be satisfied to achieve the effect; in the example, the effect is just a reachability property.
Furthermore, we want to emphasize that the concrete probabilities of the individual paths leading to
c
are important for the violation. In general, this imposes a major challenge for checking the condition (coS), for which we do not know a solution.
Similar problems arise when trying to check the existence of a co-safety SPR cause. A witness might be just one individual path, potentially only together with a scheduler that realizes this path with very low probability.

\lhd

6.2.2.
Computation of quality measures of co-safety causes

Analogously to Section
6.1.2
, we can define recall, coverage ratio, and f-score of co-safety PR causes.
With the construction of
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}
and the correspondence between schedulers
\mathfrak{S}
for
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathfrak{T}
for
\mathcal{M}_{[\mathsf{rEff},\mathsf{rCause}]}

satisfying Equations (
26
)-(
28
) established in the proof of Lemma
50
, we obtain analogously to the setting of reachability PR causes:

Corollary 52
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\mathsf{rEff}\subseteq S^{\omega}
an
\omega
-regular language given as DRA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
.
Given a co-safety SPR/GPR cause
\mathsf{rCause}\subseteq S^{\omega}
by a DFA
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rCause}}
, we can compute
\operatorname{\mathit{recall}}(\mathsf{Cause})
,
\mathit{covrat}(\mathsf{Cause})
and
\mathit{fscore}(\mathsf{Cause})
in polynomial time.

6.2.3.
Finding optimal co-safety PR causes

Already for reachability PR causes, we have seen that without further restrictions on the causes we allow, causes might be trivial and intuitively violate the idea of temporal priority (cf. Section
6.1.3
).
Hence, also here, we impose an additional condition, a variation of the condition (
TempPrio
) used above:
In line with the difference between the definitions of reachability PR causes and co-safety PR causes, we require that after any good prefix
\sigma
of a co-safety cause
\mathsf{rCause}
, the probability that effect
\mathsf{rEff}
will occur is not guaranteed to be
1
, i.e., we require that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}^{\min}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\sigma)<1\text{ for all good prefixes $\sigma$ of $\mathsf{rCause}$. }

(TempPrio2)

Unfortunately, we will observe that there are some obstacles in the way when trying to find optimal co-safety PR causes.

Following the observations from Theorem
36
we can define a canonical co-safety PR cause which is an optimal co-safety SPR cause for both recall and coverage ratio.
In this fully path-based setting this canonical cause consists of all minimal paths which are singleton co-safety SPR causes.
However, as we are not aware of a feasible way to check (coS), the computation of this cause is unclear.

For co-safety GPR causes, the following example illustrates that there might be no recall-optimal causes that respect (
TempPrio2
).
Intuitively, the reason is that causes can be pushed arbitrarily close towards a violation of the probability raising condition while increasing the recall:

\mathsf{init}
a
b
c
e
f
f
1/3
1/3
1/3
1/2
1/2
3/4
1/4
1/4
3/4

Figure 18.
Markov chain
\mathcal{M}
where the regular effect
\lozenge e
has no recall-optimal regular PR-cause.

{exa}

This example will show that there is a Markov chain
\mathcal{M}
with a state
e
such that the effect
\mathsf{rEff}=\lozenge e
has regular GPR causes that respect the condition (
TempPrio2
), but no recall-optimal co-safety GPR cause that respects (
TempPrio2
).

Consider the Markov chain
\mathcal{M}
depicted in Figure
18
with states
S
and the effect
\mathsf{rEff}=\lozenge e
.
First of all, we have that
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff})=2/3
. Furthermore, clearly the cause
\mathsf{init}\,b\,S^{\omega}
with the unique minimal good prefix
\mathsf{init}\,b
is a regular GPR cause for example
as
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\mathsf{init}\,b)=3/4
.

Next, note that there cannot be a regular GPR cause
\mathsf{rCause}^{\prime}
that does not have
\mathsf{init}\,b
as a minimal good prefix. By (TempPrio2) the minimal good prefixes are not allowed to end in
e
.
Furthermore
\mathsf{init}
is clearly also no candidate for a minimal good prefix of
\mathsf{rCause}^{\prime}
as that would imply that
\mathsf{rCause}
consists of all paths of
\mathcal{M}
and cannot satisfy the condition (
rGPR
).
If
\mathsf{init}\,b
is not a minimal good prefix, hence all minimal good prefixes have to end in
a
,
c
, or
f
. Afterwards, the probability to reach
e
is at most
1/4
and hence also the probability
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\mathsf{rCause}^{\prime})\leq 1/4
because it is a weighted average of the probabilities
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\sigma)\leq 1/4
of the minimal good prefixes
\sigma
of
\mathsf{rCause}^{\prime}
.

So, all regular GPR causes have the minimal good prefix
\mathsf{init}\,b
together with potentially further minimal good prefixes.
Let now

\mathsf{rCause}_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{init}\,b\,S^{\omega}\cup A_{p}

where
A_{p}
is a regular subset of the paths
\{\mathsf{init}\,a^{k}\,c\mid k\geq 1\}
such that all the paths in
A_{p}
together have probability mass
\frac{1}{3}p
.
Note that we can find such a set
A_{p}
for a dense set of values
p\in[0,1]
.
We compute

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause}_{p})=\frac{1}{3}(1+p)

and

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause}_{p}\land\mathsf{rEff})=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{12}p.

So, we obtain that
\mathsf{rCause}_{p}
is a regular GPR cause if

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}\mid\mathsf{rCause}_{p})=\frac{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{12}p}{\frac{1}{3}(1+p)}>\frac{2}{3}=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rEff}).

After multiplying the inequality with
12(1+p)
, we see that this holds iff
9+3p>8+8p
iff
p<\frac{1}{5}
.
The recall of
\mathsf{rCause}_{p}
is now

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{rCause}_{p}\mid\mathsf{rEff})=\frac{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{12}p}{2/3}=\frac{3}{8}+\frac{1}{8}p.

So, among the co-safety GPR causes of the form
\mathsf{rCause}_{p}
, there is no recall-optimal one. For
p
tending to
1/5
from below, the recall always increases.
Note also that an
\varepsilon
-recall-optimal co-safety GPR cause for
\varepsilon>0
must take a very complicated form. It has to select paths of the form
\mathsf{init}\,a^{k}\,c
that have probability
\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{k}}
such that there probability adds up to a value less than, but close to
\frac{1}{15}
.

\lhd

We have seen that for recall-optimal (and hence coverage ratio-optimal) SPR causes for an effect given by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
, we can provide a characterization of the canonical cause.
How to compute this cause, however, is unclear as we do not know how to check the co-safety SPR condition and as there might be some paths ending in a given state in the product of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{rEff}}
that belong to the canonical cause while other paths ending in that state do not.
For co-safety GPR causes, we have even seen that there might be no (non-trivial) optimal causes and that causes close to the optimum can be required to take a very complicated shape.
As the f-score is a more involved quality measure than the recall, we cannot expect that the search for f-score optimal causes is simpler.
It seems to be likely that the situation is at least as bad as for recall-optimal causes if not worse.

7.
Conclusion

In this work we formalized the probability-raising principle in MDPs and studied several quality notions for probability-raising causes.
We covered fundamental algorithmic problems for both the strict (local) and global view, where we considered a basic state-based setting in which cause and effect are given as sets of states.
We extended this setting to
\omega
-regular path properties as effects in two ways.
In a more simple setting we kept causes as sets of states and in a more general approach considered co-safety path properties as causes.

Strict vs. Global probability raising

In our basic setting of state-based cause-effect relations, our results indicate that GPR causes are more general overall by leaving more flexibility to achieve better quality measures, while algorithmic reasoning on SPR causes is simpler.
This changed when extending the framework by considering
\omega
-regular effects given by a deterministic Rabin automaton.
Our results mainly stem from a polynomial reduction from
\omega
-regular effects to reachability effects (Lemma
42
).
The caveat here is that the strict PR condition translates to a global PR condition after this transformation, which increases the algorithmic complexity of reachability SPR causes to the level of reachability GPR causes.
Thus, the strict probability-raising loses its advantage over the global perspective.
Furthermore, when considering causes as co-safety path properties we observe increasing difficulties to handle strict probability-raising.
This stems from an underlying problem in the approach of strict probability-raising applied to path properties.
As we consider cause-effect relations between these properties, it is somewhat unnatural to require each individual path to raise the probability of the effect property.
Rather, it is more natural to say a path property as a whole causes another one, instead of saying all possible realizations of a path property cause another one.
This means that co-safety GPR causes also seem more natural than co-safety SPR causes from a philosophical standpoint.

Non-strict inequality in the PR conditions

The approach of probability-raising within this work is in line with the classical notion in literature that uses a strict inequality in the PR condition.
As a consequence causes might not exist (see Example
3.1
).
However, relaxing the PR condition by only requiring a non-strict inequality would apparently be a minor change that broadens the choice of causes.
Indeed, the proposed algorithms for checking the SPR and GPR condition for reachability effects (Section
4
) can easily be modified for the relaxed definition.
As the algorithms of both extended settings discussed in Section
6
stem mainly from a reduction to reachability effects this also holds for reachability and co-safety causes of regular effects.
However, a non-strict inequality in the PR condition would lead to a questionable notion of causality, as e.g.
\{\mathsf{init}\}
would always be a recall- and ratio-optimal cause.
Thus, other side constraints are needed in order to make use of the relaxed PR condition.
E.g., requiring the non-strict inequality for all schedulers that reach a cause with positive probability and also requiring the existence of a witnessing scheduler for the PR condition with strict inequality might be a useful alternative definition which agrees with Def.
3
for Markov chains.

Relaxing the minimality condition

As many causality notions in the literature include some minimality constraint, we included the condition
\mathrm{Pr}^{\max}_{\mathcal{M}}(\neg\mathsf{Cause}\,\mathrm{U}\,c)>0
for all states of
\mathsf{Cause}
in the state-based setting and for reachability PR causes of regular effects.
However, this requirement could be dropped without affecting the algorithmic results presented here.
This can be useful when the task is to identify components or agents which are responsible for the occurrences of undesired effects.
In these cases the cause candidates are fixed (e.g., for each agent
i
, the set of states controlled by agent
i
), but some of them might violate the minimality condition.

Future directions

In this work we considered type-like causality where cause-effect relations are defined within the model without needing an actual execution that shows the effect.
Hence, causes are considered in a forward-looking manner.
Notions of probabilistic backward causality that take a concrete execution of the system into account and considerations on PR causality with external interventions as in Pearl’s do-calculus
[
Pea09
]
are left for future work.
